| 4.35 | Adaptive idle heartbeats (battery) | Implemented locally, NOT deployed, NOT pushed | New bonded_core::heartbeat policy: fast cadence while user traffic flows or a path is suspect, back off after 60s idle to 25s pings on the active path only; standby paths go silent while idle (server reaps them after 30s; re-pinged and evicted if dead, then re-dialed by the supervisor, when traffic resumes). Applied in the Linux CLI worker (5s active / 25s idle, pong-silence threshold 3x interval: 15s/75s) and the Android worker (10s active / 25s idle). QUIC keep_alive 10s -> 25s in both the client config and the peer-relay listener config (quinn applies it at setup with no dynamic adjustment; 25s stays under the server's 30s transport reap and typical NAT timeouts). Tradeoff documented in heartbeat.rs: 25s leaves only 5s slack vs the 30s server reap, so a Doze stall can kill even the active path; standby failover during idle may briefly try a reaped standby and fall through via existing send-failure handling. Verified: cargo check clean (1 pre-existing unused_mut warning in peer_runtime.rs); bonded-core tests 46/46 incl. 2 new heartbeat tests; bonded-client lib 57 passed / 7 failed — the same 7 QUIC/h3/peer-relay tests fail on clean HEAD (sandbox blocks UDP), verified by stash comparison; bonded-ffi lib 17 passed / 1 failed — android_session_runtime_can_pair_and_exchange_packets panics on Rustls CryptoProvider, same failure on clean HEAD. |
| 4.36 | Session monitor + home poll backoff (battery) | Implemented locally, NOT deployed, NOT pushed | BondedVpnService session monitor no longer serializes a snapshot every 1s forever: it polls every 1s only while the Flutter UI is asking for the snapshot (getSessionSnapshot records a demand timestamp on every UI request; the monitor treats demand within 5s, or the first 60s after start, as foreground) and backs off to 30s between serializations otherwise. State-change events (tile/widget refresh, error recovery) still fire on the next poll, up to 30s late in the background. Home screen 1s poll now skips when the app is not resumed or this route is not current (foreground behaviour unchanged). Verified: flutter analyze clean, flutter test 19/19 incl. 1 new widget test (polling continues foregrounded, stops backgrounded, resumes on foreground). Kotlin validated by the slice-5 AAB build (v242, 92.3 MB, Gradle 856.9 s, 2026-11-05). |
| 4.37 | Multicast lock only when peer sharing is on (battery) | Implemented locally, NOT deployed, NOT pushed | BondedVpnService acquired the Wi-Fi multicast lock unconditionally on VPN start, keeping the Wi-Fi driver awake for every multicast frame on the LAN for the whole session. The lock is now acquired only when the paired server's peerShareEnabled flag is on (peer discovery is the only thing that needs it; the peer runtime only starts when the flag is on). Release path is unchanged and already returns early when no lock is held. No new test: the change is a one-line gate on an existing flag in service start code that needs the Android framework; verified by review; Kotlin validated by the slice-5 AAB build (v242, 2026-11-05). |
| 4.38 | Windowed multicast peer discovery (battery) | Implemented locally, NOT deployed, NOT pushed | Kotlin PeerDiscoveryWindowScheduler holds the lock only in a 10s window at the top of each UTC minute (same device clock the Rust side uses, so both sides agree), plus a 30s burst on VPN start and on network path change, plus a 60s sweep every 30 min of windowed operation. The sweep is the fallback for both windowing failure modes (skewed/unsynced clock, or windows that never overlap): a full minute overlaps any peer window regardless of skew, with no need to detect NTP sync (not reliably observable) and no Rust-to-Kotlin discovery signalling. Rust: provider re-announces at each UTC minute (unregister then register; goodbye resend at +120ms lands before the probed announce at ~+750ms, daemon never restarted); consumer re-queries at each UTC minute via a 1s recv timeout that notices the new minute (required because mdns-sd backs a long browse off to one query per hour); the re-query runs between recv calls, never during one, since it replaces the receiver. Cached peers bypass the window by design (verified: introductions are HTTPS to the server, relay dials are unicast QUIC, redials/renewals use the cached advertisement). The 5s advertise-IP poll is unchanged except for the added minute check; QR pairing and server settings untouched. Verified: cargo check clean (1 pre-existing warning elsewhere), bonded-client peer_discovery tests 2/2, flutter test 19/19, flutter analyze clean. Kotlin reviewed by hand; Kotlin unit tests added for the window math (PeerDiscoveryWindowTest); Kotlin validated by the slice-5 AAB build (v242, 2026-11-05). (The Gradle test run itself was not run separately — the AAB build compiles all Kotlin.) |
| 4.39 | Battery-saver mode (battery) | Implemented locally, NOT deployed, NOT pushed | New AppSettings.batterySaver (default OFF, Settings toggle, SharedPreferences via the existing get/updateAppSettings channel) plumbed through BondedVpnService.nativeStartSession into ClientConfig.battery_saver. When ON: (a) the effective protocol list is reordered WireGuard-first (prefer_wireguard_first, applied to the server-provided list or the default; OFF leaves the order untouched); (b) the FFI worker auto-parks cellular paths while the session is idle (60 s, same definition as the heartbeat) AND a non-cellular path is live — parked paths go last in send order via a separate auto_paused_paths set, so debug long-press pause can never collide with it. Cellular paths are identified by bind address: Kotlin passes the cellular bind list at session start and on every path update (new nativeUpdatePaths param; AndroidNetworkPathManager.cellularBindAddresses). Auto-park clears immediately on an outbound traffic burst and on the next tick when Wi-Fi is gone, so failover still works — with the documented tradeoff: resuming cellular takes a moment (re-spread on next burst / re-dial) instead of being instant. HONEST LIMIT: parked cellular still sends QUIC keepalive PINGs every 25 s, because quinn 0.11 fixes keepalive at connect time with no runtime toggle; session heartbeats and data are stopped, but the radio is not fully asleep. Full radio sleep needs tearing the cellular transport down and re-dialing on demand — a larger supervisor change, deliberately deferred, not claimed. Verified: cargo check --tests clean, new FFI unit tests 4/4 (order, policy, address matching), flutter analyze clean, flutter test 19/19. Kotlin reviewed by hand; validated by the slice-5 AAB build (v242, 2026-11-05). |
| 4.40 | Packet-path batching (battery) | Implemented locally, NOT deployed, NOT pushed | Outbound fast path: the shared 32 KB read buffer is now handed straight to nativeHandleTunOutbound with its length, and the JNI copies exactly that many bytes via GetByteArrayRegion — previously the JVM copied the packet (copyOf) AND native converted the whole 32 KB array per packet. A JVM copy remains only for the pending queue, which must own the packet past the call. Inbound fallback: the per-packet flush() is removed (FileOutputStream is unbuffered, so flush was already a no-op; TUN writes are packet-atomic, so write batching is impossible — each packet needs its own write syscall). The drain thread now exits once the native fd handoff is active AND the residual queue is drained, instead of spinning on the 1 ms poll forever after a successful re-handoff; the exit path deliberately does not close the stream, because it wraps the live TUN fd the outbound thread still reads from (a later handoff failure restarts the thread). Already-satisfied, verified not changed: the handoff outcome is logged at session start, and the 1 ms fallback only starts when the handoff actually failed (both start sites guard on it); the direct Rust-to-TUN path is untouched. LEFT OUT per the task's proof bar: a Bytes-queue refactor to remove the inbound payload to_vec copy — it cannot be test-verified here (the worker integration test fails pre-existing on the Rustls CryptoProvider issue) and one copy at the JNI ownership boundary is fundamental anyway. Verified: cargo check --tests clean, flutter analyze clean, flutter test 19/19. Kotlin reviewed by hand; validated by the slice-5 AAB build (v242, 2026-11-05). |
| 4.41 | Transport overhead diagnostic (battery) | Implemented locally, NOT deployed, NOT pushed | New diagnostic in the Network Tests screen. Wire counters: bonded-core `transport.rs` gains `WireStats { tx_bytes, rx_bytes }` + `Transport::wire_stats()` (default None); NaiveTcp counts 4-byte prefix + encoded frame bytes on send/recv; QUIC reads quinn `ConnectionStats.udp_tx/udp_rx.bytes` (bytes inside UDP datagrams, IP/UDP headers excluded); WireGuard counts socket send/recv via a single `send_datagram()` helper; WebSocketTls stays None — tungstenite owns the TCP+TLS stack behind a closed Connector enum, so no socket hook exists, and reporting WS message bytes would understate overhead (documented in code, model, and UI). New bonded-core `overhead.rs`: `compute_overhead()` (saturating wire-minus-inside, null ratios when inside is zero, zero elapsed gives zero bandwidths) + 4 unit tests. `ClientTransport::wire_stats()` passthrough (PeerRelay delegates to inner Quic). FFI: `AndroidPathStat` gains optional wire fields, filled live at publish and refreshed on every worker tick (publish alone would go stale between structural changes), serialized as `wireTxBytes`/`wireRxBytes` (null when unmeasured); Dart `PathStat` parses them as nullable. UI: `TransportOverheadResult` model (mirrors overhead.rs, incl. counters-reset detection when a transport re-dials mid-window) + `TransportOverheadCard` + a Network Tests section with a payload-size selector (256 KiB / 1 MiB / 10 MiB, default 1 MiB), a per-transport wire-status table, a live 10 s window measurement (snapshot, wait, snapshot; labeled as real traffic, payload unknown so inside bytes stand in for it), and a DISABLED fixed-payload Run button. NOT DONE (honest): the live fixed-payload TCP-through-tunnel run is not wired — it needs a TCP echo endpoint on the tunnel gateway 10.8.0.1, which is NOT implemented and NOT deployed. The server insertion point is documented (`SmoltcpForwarder::dispatch_tcp`, mirroring the UDP DNS special-case), but TCP echo is stateful termination through smoltcp, a multi-hundred-line change to the production forwarding path with no live test available, so it was deferred rather than built blind. The measurement plumbing IS proven: new bonded-client test `transport_overhead_diagnostic_measures_fixed_payload` sends a fixed 256 KiB payload through a single naive_tcp transport against the loopback test server and asserts exact framing (wire = inside + frames × (4 + header)) and overhead < 5%. Verified: bonded-core 52/52 (incl. 2 new transport counter tests + 4 overhead tests), bonded-client diagnostic test passes (59 passed / 7 failed overall — same pre-existing sandbox-UDP set), bonded-ffi 21 passed / 1 failed (same pre-existing CryptoProvider failure), cargo check -p bonded-ffi --tests clean, flutter analyze clean, flutter test 27/27 (incl. 8 new overhead tests: model math, null-wire, zero-elapsed, reset, WSS status, card rendering, live-window screen flow). | 4.42 | Tunnel-gateway TCP echo endpoint (10.8.0.1:18099) | DEPLOYED to Dell (localhost/bonded-server:echo, image 804b80f89101, binary SHA 331e69ba, 2026-11-05), NOT pushed | Server terminates TCP to the tunnel gateway echo port locally instead of forwarding it: each SmoltcpForwarder binds one loopback listener (127.0.0.1, ephemeral port) at construction and spawns an accept loop; dispatch_tcp dials that listener through the normal dial-first path for echo SYNs, so the whole smoltcp bridge (SYN-ACK only after dial success, half-close, RST on failure) is reused unchanged and the echo is just another upstream TcpStream. Reachable ONLY via the tunnel; nothing new listens on a public interface; forwarding for every other destination unchanged; DNS proxy, dial-first, and the IPv6 ingest gate untouched. Port 18099 chosen to collide with nothing (53 DNS, 853 DoT, 8080-8082 health/status, 48117 TV QR listener, 443 public). Limits: 16 concurrent connections per listener, 64 MiB echoed per connection (partial echo of the crossing chunk, then close), 30 s idle timeout; debug-level logging only. Listener bind failure degrades gracefully (echo SYNs follow the normal path and RST). Smoltcp-mode only, like the DNS proxy — kernel-TUN mode bypasses the forwarder. Tests: 4 tunnel_echo unit tests (verbatim multi-segment echo, empty close, byte cap, max connections) + 2 server integration tests through the real server binary (32 KiB patterned payload in 1400-byte segments echoed byte-for-byte, second chunk on the same connection, empty close + third connection still echoes; non-echo gateway port 18098 RSTs, never SYN-ACKs). Test-harness lessons: the fake client must ACK echoed data promptly (else the server RTO fires and retransmits, which the byte comparison would count twice) and must skip stale frames when reading a SYN-ACK (leftover bare ACKs/FIN-ACKs from an earlier connection on the same session). Verified: bonded-server bin suite 104 passed / 4 failed — the same pre-existing sandbox-blocked set as clean HEAD (ICMP echo, UDP echo, 2 WireGuard; verified by stash comparison). |

Peer relays cannot be paused by index, so the note says so. Live 10 s window measurement unchanged. Tests: 4 `tcp_echo_client` tests (loopback echo 256 KiB + 1 MiB end-to-end, unreachable → EchoUnavailableException, zero-echo → EchoMismatchException) + 2 widget tests (run renders NaiveTCP + QUIC cards with 1.00 MB payloads and pauses/resumes each other path; unreachable connect shows the 'not available on this server' note and no cards). Widget-test lesson (kept): real socket I/O stalls in the fake async zone — the echo server must be created inside `tester.runAsync` (real zone) so it accepts/echoes in real time, and the tap must be followed by interleaved `runAsync(1 s)` + `pump` cycles (real I/O events fire in runAsync, handler continuations drain on pumps); pumps alone never deliver socket data, and a server created in the fake zone never accepts. Verified: flutter analyze clean, flutter test 33/33; bonded-core 52/52, bonded-server 104 passed / 4 failed (same pre-existing sandbox set), bonded-client 59 passed / 7 failed (same pre-existing sandbox set). NOT done: live run through a real tunnel — see 4.44. |

| 4.47 | Session grading on the main screen (live + last-session grade) | REMOVED 2026-11-17 (Tom's decision: grades are per-transport only, 4.50). Was implemented + UPLOADED Play internal v251 (status=completed, supersedes v249) 2026-11-06; NOT deployed (client-only), NOT pushed | No grading or session-history feature existed. Dart-only slice: new `SessionGradeTracker` + `SessionGrade` (`lib/models/session_grade.dart`) fed from the home screen's EXISTING 1 s poll tick via `_sampleGraphs` (no new timers, wakeups, permissions, server changes). Rubric (weights sum to 100): Latency 30 — session median RTT of the active path, 100 at ≤ 50 ms, 0 at ≥ 500 ms, linear; Stability 30 — path flaps per hour (active-path set changing, or a path's QUIC PING counter dropping = re-dial; max 1 event per sample), 100 at 0/h, 0 at ≥ 12/h, linear, scored only after ≥ 60 s sampled time; Availability 20 — share of sampled time with ≥ 1 active path, scored only after ≥ 10 s; Bandwidth 20 — median combined (up + down) throughput over ACTIVE windows only (a window counts only when bytes moved in it, so idle windows never drag the score down — the per-window form of the 60 s idle definition), 100 at ≥ 10 Mbps, 0 at ≤ 0.1 Mbps, linear. Letters: 90+ A, 80+ B, 70+ C, 60+ D, below 60 F, no plus/minus. Medians come from bounded histograms (RTT buckets ≤50/100/200/350/500 ms; throughput buckets 0.1/1/5/10 Mbps); the card shows the BAND ("median 50–100 ms"), never a fake-precise number, and scores interpolate from the bucket midpoint. Every component is optional at runtime: no data yet → excluded, total rebalances proportionally (Σ w·s / Σ w). Intervals > 35 s are skipped as unknown time (app backgrounded / home screen left) — 35 s admits the 30 s background cadence; flaps between samples are missed at that cadence (documented). Card (`SessionGradeCard`, one `FocusableRow` stop) sits after the connected block, near Totals/Relayed: letter + total + the four sub-scores with their measurements, a "based on N samples over M" line, and "battery is not scored". Live while connected; on disconnect the final grade is retained as "Last session" until the next session starts (in-memory only — NO history store exists in the app, and none was built in this slice; session history is a follow-up). User-directed rubric changes 2026-11-06: battery EXCLUDED from the grade entirely (no mAh/h in the score, no battery data wired into the grader; 4.46 profiler + Battery card untouched) — weights went 30/30/20/20 → 35/35/30, then bandwidth ADDED as the fourth component at 20, rebalancing to 30/30/20/20 (bandwidth in place of the removed battery component). | 4.48 | Forward wireTxBytes/wireRxBytes from Kotlin to Dart (4.41 overhead card wire rows) | Implemented + tested locally; UPLOADED Play internal v252 (status=completed, supersedes v251) 2026-11-06; NOT deployed (client-only, no server change), NOT pushed | Bug found during the 4.46 verification: Kotlin never forwarded `wireTxBytes`/`wireRxBytes` to Dart — `SessionPathStat` had no such fields and `getSessionSnapshot()` omitted them — so the 4.41 overhead card's wire rows could only ever show "not measured" on a real device, even though the Rust snapshot JSON already carried both keys and Dart's `SessionStatus.fromMap` already parsed them. No Rust changes (verified the FFI JSON renders both keys, number or literal null, from `Option<u64>` wire stats), no UI changes (the 4.41 card already renders the values when present), no new permissions, no server changes. Testability: `parseSessionPaths` + the per-path map builder were extracted from `BondedVpnService` into top-level `internal` functions (`parseSessionPaths`, `sessionPathStatToMap`) in the same file — the service class cannot be instantiated in a local unit test, and the extractions are behavior-preserving (call sites unchanged in shape). Tests: new `SessionSnapshotTest` (6: parse with wire bytes present, explicit null, null array → empty, non-object entry skipped, map forward with values, map forward with nulls); `testImplementation("org.json:json:20240303")` added — the android.jar org.json classes are stubs that throw in local unit tests, so the parse test needs the real implementation; test scope only, never ships. Dart: `session_status_test.dart` already put wire bytes in its fixture map but never asserted them — assertions added (values + nulls + legacy). Suites: flutter analyze clean, flutter test 70/70 (unchanged count — assertions added to an existing test); Kotlin unit 40/40 BUILD SUCCESSFUL (was 34/34; +6 new). | 4.49 | QUIC packet-loss measurement + display (Paths card) | Implemented + tested locally; UPLOADED Play internal v253 (status=completed, supersedes v252) 2026-11-06; NOT deployed (client-only, no server change), NOT pushed | Goal is to SEE the numbers first — measure + display only, no grade change. Quinn 0.11 `PathStats` (verified in the local cargo registry source) counts `lost_packets` / `lost_bytes` / `sent_packets` per path; a lost QUIC packet is retransmitted by quinn, so `lost_packets` is effectively the retransmit count. Loss rate = lost / sent; null while sent is zero. Rust: `WireStats` gains `quic_lost_packets` / `quic_lost_bytes` / `quic_sent_packets` as `Option<u64>` — `None` for transports that cannot observe loss (NaiveTCP, WireGuard; WebSocketTls reports no wire stats at all), so a non-QUIC path reports null, never a fabricated 0. QUIC populates them from `connection.stats().path` on the existing per-tick wire-stats pass. FFI: `AndroidPathStat` gains the three `Option<u64>`s, set in `publish_path_stats` and refreshed on the per-tick pass (same place that refreshes wire bytes + PING frames), JSON keys `quicLostPackets` / `quicLostBytes` / `quicSentPackets` (null when unmeasured). Kotlin: `SessionPathStat` gains nullable Longs; `parseSessionPaths` + `sessionPathStatToMap` forward them (same isNull → null / optLong pattern as `quicPingFrames`). Dart: `PathStat` gains nullable ints + `lossRate` (lost / sent, null when unmeasured or sent == 0) + `lossDisplay` ("3.0%" one decimal, "—" when null); `SessionStatus` gains aggregate `lossRate` / `lossDisplay` (totals over the paths that observe loss). Display: per-path "loss X%" token in the Paths card detail line next to RTT, and the same token on peer-relay rows (relays are QUIC, so they have the data); session aggregate in the Paths card header when any path observes loss. Non-QUIC / no-data paths show "loss —", never a number. No grade changes, no new permissions, no server changes, no new timers, no TCP_INFO work (TCP retransmits are a separate slice), no WireGuard loss inference. Tests: new `wire_stats_quic_loss_defaults_to_none` (core), FFI `snapshot_json_carries_wakeup_counters` extended (loss keys present + null); Kotlin `SessionSnapshotTest` extended (parse present / explicit null / forward values / forward nulls); Dart `session_status_test.dart` extended (parse + lossRate math incl. sent == 0 → null + "—" display + legacy). Suites: flutter analyze clean, flutter test 71/71 (was 70/70; +1 new sent==0 test); core 54/0 (was 53/0; +1 new); FFI 22/1 (same pre-existing rustls CryptoProvider panic, message-confirmed; stash-verified identical on the clean tree); Kotlin unit 40/40 BUILD SUCCESSFUL (assertions extended in existing tests, no new test fns; same count as the 4.48 baseline). Note: three earlier Gradle attempts died at the daemon fork with empty client logs while the v251 worktree AAB build ran concurrently — root cause found in the delayed run's output: lock timeout on the shared flutter_tools `buildOutputCleanup` cache held by the v251 Gradle instance; the daemon logs showed the build 'executing' with no task lines. Re-ran cleanly once the v251/v252 builds finished: BUILD SUCCESSFUL in 6m 21s. Open: live device check (loss % moving on a real QUIC session; TCP/WG rows showing "—"); whether loss belongs in the 4.47 grade (decision pending seeing real numbers). | 4.50 | Per-transport grades on the Paths card | Implemented + tested locally; UPLOADED Play internal v259 (status=completed, supersedes v253) 2026-11-06; NOT deployed (client-only, no server change), NOT pushed | User correction of 4.47: grades belong on the TRANSPORTS (per path), not the session. Dart-only slice: new `TransportGradeTracker` + `TransportGrade` (`lib/models/transport_grade.dart`) fed from the home screen's EXISTING 1 s poll tick via `_sampleGraphs`, alongside the session tracker (no new timers, wakeups, permissions, server changes, no Rust/Kotlin — every input already arrives per path in the snapshot). Rubric (weights sum to 100, same shape as 4.47): Latency 30 — this path's median RTT, 100 at ≤ 50 ms, 0 at ≥ 500 ms, linear (4.47 function reused); Stability 30 — this path's flaps per hour (its QUIC PING counter dropping = re-dial, or the path rejoining after being inactive; max 1 event per sample), 100 at 0/h, 0 at ≥ 12/h, linear (4.47 function reused), scored only after ≥ 60 s sampled time for that path; Loss 20 — this path's QUIC loss rate (4.49 counters), 100 at ≤ 0.1%, 0 at ≥ 10%, linear, scored only after ≥ 100 packets sent (smaller denominator is not a measurement; non-QUIC paths never get a loss score, never a fabricated 0); Bandwidth 20 — 100 UNLESS saturation is observed, per the user-directed rule: absolute throughput is NOT scored (a lightly-used path is not a slow path), so the 4.47 absolute-bandwidth bands were deliberately NOT reused. A window counts as active when bytes moved in it; it counts as saturated when, under that load, RTT inflates against the path's own low-water mark (≥ 1.5× and ≥ +100 ms) or the interval loss rate spikes (≥ 5% over ≥ 20 packets sent). Score = 100 × (1 − saturated / active), scored only after ≥ 10 active windows (before that the component is excluded, not assumed 100 with no data). NOT implemented: throughput plateauing while demand exists — there is no demand signal in the snapshot (no per-path queue depth), so a plateau cannot be distinguished from light use; RTT inflation + loss spikes under load are the observable saturation signals. No per-path availability component: a path may legitimately sit inactive while another carries the session, so availability would penalize failover (loss takes availability's 20). Reuse, not duplication: the RTT histogram buckets/median/band label + `bucketIndex` were promoted from private to public in `session_grade.dart` and are shared; `latencyScoreFromMedianRtt`, `stabilityScoreFromFlapsPerHour`, `letterForScore`, `totalScore`, `WeightedComponent`, `formatSampledFor` are reused; `PathStat` gains a `gradeKey` getter (`dN` / `r<peerId>:<kind>`) shared by both trackers. Display: each direct-path row and each peer-relay leg shows a letter + score chip (`TransportGradeChip`, e.g. "B · 84") next to the Active chip; tapping a graded row opens a bottom-sheet detail view (`TransportGradeDetail`) with the four sub-scores + measurements and a "based on N samples over M" line stating the bandwidth rule. Paths with no grade yet show no chip and do not open a sheet. The 4.47 session-grade card was REMOVED 2026-11-17 (Tom's decision); its shared scoring primitives moved to `lib/models/grade_scoring.dart`. whether to keep or remove it is a follow-up question for the user (it now duplicates the per-path grades in session form). This also answers 4.49's open question: loss belongs in the transport grade, not the session grade. | 4.51 | Traffic modes framework + rules (default effective; other modes selectable with fallback) | Implemented + tested locally; UPLOADED Play internal v259 (status=completed, supersedes v253) 2026-11-06; NOT deployed (client-only, no server change), NOT pushed | Modes enum with five variants (`default`, `best_transport`, `lowest_latency`, `bandwidth_boost`, `redundant`); only `default` has behavior in this slice — the other three land in 4.52–4.54, and until then selecting one falls back to default behavior, stated in the UI next to every picker (never silently pretended). Rust: new `bonded-core` `traffic_mode.rs` — `TrafficMode` (wire names snake_case, unknown names deserialize to `default`), `TrafficRule` (id/kind/target/resolved_ips/mode), `TrafficModeEngine` (default mode + rules, JSON `{"defaultMode": …, "rules": […]}` shared with Dart). Matching is destination-IP only, most-specific wins: exact IP > subnet (longer prefix first) > domain-resolved IPs, ties to the earliest rule; malformed targets and unresolved domain rules match nothing; a destination matching no rule falls back to the default mode. `order_candidates(mode, flow_hash, count, active_index, signals)` is the one ordering entry point both pin points use; a `PathSignal` struct (RTT/loss/grade, all optional) is defined now so 4.52–4.54 add ordering arms without changing the signature. Engine wired into BOTH flow-pin points: the Linux client loop (`run_linux_packet_loop`, default engine — the CLI has no rule input yet) and the Android worker (engine snapshot per packet, ordered before the frame is built since building moves the packet); `order_candidates_for_send` is replaced by `demote_paused_paths` (the engine owns order, the FFI layer only demotes paused paths, preserving mode order within each group) — with the default engine the try-list is exactly today's hash rotation. Rules plumbing (see Key Implementation Decisions): rules live in Rust (global `RwLock<TrafficModeEngine>` in bonded-ffi), set at session start from a new `trafficRulesJson` parameter on `nativeStartSession` and live-updated via a new `nativeUpdateTrafficRules` JNI (MainActivity `updateTrafficRules` → `BondedVpnService.updateTrafficRules` → native; false when no session is running, stored table applies on next connect); malformed JSON falls back to the default engine, never breaks the session. Dart owns persistence + UI + resolution: `TrafficModeSettings` lives in `AppSettings` as one JSON blob through the EXISTING get/updateAppSettings channel into SharedPreferences (`traffic_rules_json`) — no second store. UI: new `TrafficRulesScreen` (`/traffic-rules`, entry in Settings under Traffic routing with a default-mode + rule-count summary) — default-mode dropdown with the fallback note, rules list (mode + resolved IPs, unresolved rules flagged in red as matching nothing), add/edit dialog with kind auto-detection (IP > CIDR > domain) and resolve-on-save, delete with confirm. Domain rules are PRE-RESOLVED on-device (explicit user direction, no DNS snooping): `InternetAddress.lookup` when the rule is saved AND again on VPN start (home screen connect path, before `startBackgroundService`); a failed re-resolution keeps the stored IPs (a transient DNS failure must not un-match traffic); boot/tile/widget starts without the UI use the last-saved IPs. RESOLVER DEPENDENCE, proven 2026-11-06: `listen.mixlr.com` (tonight's redundant test target — the actual Winterhawks stream host; `winterhawks.codingwell.net` is just the player page, corrected the same evening) resolves to 15.235.46.15 on the user's network but 198.18.232.237 from this VM's resolver — the same name yields different IPs per network, which is why resolution must happen on-device and match ALL returned IPs. Neither IP is hardcoded anywhere in app code; tests use the user's observed IP only as literal input data to the matcher (the permitted form), never as a baked-in resolution. Server side unchanged: return flows still pin by flow hash; server-side modes are a 4.52–4.54 follow-up. HONEST LIMIT for tonight: a `redundant` rule for `listen.mixlr.com` can be created now and WILL match the stream's traffic, but its behavior is default spreading until 4.54 implements redundant (both directions). Tests: new `traffic_mode_test.dart` (model JSON, wire names, validation, CIDR ranges) + `traffic_rules_service_test.dart` (4: live on-device resolution of `listen.mixlr.com` asserting only that it returns usable IPs — never which ones — unresolvable-domain flagging, IP/subnet passthrough, re-resolution keeps stored IPs) + `traffic_rules_screen_test.dart` (5 widget tests incl. add-IP-rule save + live push) + Rust `traffic_mode` tests (14: specificity, longest-prefix, first-wins, unresolved/malformed match nothing, JSON round-trip + bad-JSON fallback, default order + fallback equivalence, `listen.mixlr.com` rule matching the user's observed IP as test input, multi-IP matching, unmatched destination falls back to default) + Kotlin `TrafficRulesUpdateTest` (1: push returns false with no service instance). | 4.52 | Best transport + lowest latency modes (incl. server return half) | Implemented + tested locally; UPLOADED Play internal v259 (status=completed) 2026-11-06; DEPLOYED to Dell as localhost/bonded-server:redundant 2026-11-06 (container up, health 200, binary SHA verified in-container), NOT pushed | BEST TRANSPORT: `order_candidates` gains a `best_transport` arm — ranking is grade first (4.50 per-transport total, higher wins), then RTT (lower wins), then loss rate (lower wins); a missing signal at any step sorts after any measured one (unknown is never good, never a fabricated 0), and the transport index breaks remaining ties so the order is deterministic for a given signal set. Deterministic-in-signals IS the pinning: a flow stays on its first candidate while the transport set and the signals are stable, with no per-packet re-pinning and no per-flow state in the engine; the flow hash is ignored for this mode. Degrades gracefully: no grades yet → RTT decides; no RTT yet either → loss decides; nothing measured → index order (every flow starts on transport 0 until the first heartbeat pong or grade push). LOWEST LATENCY: its own arm, a single metric — RTT, lower wins — grade and loss never enter the comparison, so a high-grade slow path loses to a low-grade fast one; missing RTT sorts after measured RTT; nothing measured → index order. Signals are built per packet in the Android worker (`path_signals`): RTT from the last heartbeat pong (`last_rtt_ms`, 0 = unmeasured), QUIC loss rate from the cumulative counters (lost/sent, none while sent is zero or for transports that cannot observe loss), grade from the live Dart push. Grades cross the JNI boundary on the home screen's existing 1 s poll tick: new `PathGradesService.pushLive` (`updatePathGrades`, bare JSON array in snapshot paths-list order, one total per transport vec index, null when ungraded) through MainActivity → `BondedVpnService.updatePathGrades` → `nativeUpdatePathGrades` JNI into a global `RwLock<Vec<Option<u8>>>`; Kotlin is a string passthrough like the rule table. Session start clears the grades (a new session never ranks on the previous session's paths); malformed JSON clears rather than keeping stale grades. The Linux client loop is unchanged: it still runs the default engine (no rule input, no grades), so the new arms are inert there — CLI rule input is still open from 4.51. RETURN HALF (server): return traffic now comes back on the transport the flow's most recent uplink data arrived on. New `bonded-core` `flow_key` hashes the two endpoint (address, port) pairs in canonical order, so uplink and reply share a key — `flow_hash` is per-direction and cannot match directions. `SessionOutboundRouter::note_frame` now takes the frame (7 call sites updated: bootstrap ×2, main ×2, quic ×2, wireguard ×1) and records flow → transport on data frames only (PING/PONG never claim a flow; unparseable packets have no key); `deliver` prefers the recorded transport when it is still live, else the existing hash pin. The map is bounded at 4096 entries (arbitrary single-entry eviction when full; the evicted flow re-learns on its next uplink packet) and pruned on `remove_transport` / `adopt_epoch` / `retire_by_peer`. The non-multipath `deliver` branch (last-active first) is unchanged. UI: `isImplemented` is now true for best transport and lowest latency, so the fallback note ("Not live yet — falls back to Default until …") shows only for `bandwidthBoost` (4.53) and `redundant` (4.54), in both the rules screen pickers and the Settings summary — no screen text was hand-edited, it follows the model. Tests: Rust `traffic_mode` tests (7 new: grade-first ranking, RTT-then-loss tiebreaks, unknown-sorts-last, lowest-latency RTT-only incl. grade-ignored, `order_for_packet` through a rule) + `flow_key` direction test (1 new) + server router tests (3 new: return on last-used, re-pin on uplink move, hash-pin fallback with no uplink / dead transport, PING does not claim a flow) + FFI `path_grades_parse_and_signal_build` (1 new: JSON parse, malformed clears, signal build incl. padding) + Dart `path_grades_service_test.dart` (4 new: JSON shape, live push, false result, channel failure) + Kotlin `PathGradesUpdateTest` (1 new: push returns false with no service instance) + `traffic_mode_test.dart` `isImplemented` expectations updated. Suites: flutter analyze clean, flutter test 117/117 (was 113/113; +4 new); core 75/0 (was 68/0; +7 new); server 107/4 (stash baseline 104/4 — same 4 pre-existing sandbox-UDP failures, 2 server_integration + 2 wireguard, "Operation not permitted"; +3 new); client 61/7 (identical pre-existing failures, stash-verified, untouched by this slice); ffi 23/1 (stash baseline 22/1 — same pre-existing `android_session_runtime_can_pair_and_exchange_packets` failure; +1 new); Kotlin unit 42/42 BUILD SUCCESSFUL via `:app:testDebugUnitTest` (was 41/41; +1 new; 6m 53s). | 4.54 | Redundant mode (duplicate on all transports, both ways) | Implemented + tested locally; UPLOADED Play internal v259 (status=completed) 2026-11-06; DEPLOYED to Dell as localhost/bonded-server:redundant 2026-11-06 (carries the 4.52 return half + 4.54 server duplication; binary SHA `ef0a634a…` verified in-container), NOT pushed | CLIENT (Android worker): the mode is resolved per packet via a new `TrafficModeEngine::mode_for_packet` (the engine already resolved it inside `order_for_packet`; the pin point needs it separately to flag the frame). A redundant flow is NOT pinned — duplication replaces pinning. `order_candidates` gains a `redundant` arm returning every transport in index order (flow hash and signals ignored — the fan-out set is the same for every packet of the flow); the worker fans the frame out to all live transports via a new `redundant_fan_out_targets` (the healthy prefix of the demoted candidate list — paused paths NEVER receive a duplicate, debug pause and battery-saver auto-pause alike). A failed target is removed with the same bookkeeping as the try-list (path-lost notice, snapshot sync) and the fan-out CONTINUES on the rest; a success does not stop the loop. All-paused degrades to the ordinary try-list (the packet still goes out once on a paused path, never dropped); single transport sends exactly one copy. The frame carries the new `FLAG_REDUNDANT` (0x0040, `bonded-core` `session.rs`) so the server knows to fan the return traffic out. STATS: session-level outbound packets/bytes count the user packet once; each path that carried a copy is credited that copy's bytes (per-path bytes show the N× cost, session bytes do not). LINUX CLIENT LOOP: the same fan-out branch (its default engine makes it inert until CLI rule input lands — the two pin points must not drift) and the same flag on its frames; its receive path now treats a stale sequence as a benign drop instead of ending the session (`ingest_inbound` StaleSequence → debug log + continue; ConnectionMismatch still fatal) — a fanned-out downlink's second copy routinely arrives after the first was consumed, and the old `?` would have killed the session on it. SERVER — HOW IT LEARNS A FLOW IS REDUNDANT: the flag IS the signaling. The server has no rule table, so it learns redundancy only from the client's frames: one bit on outbound data frames the client already sends — no new control message, no extra round trip, no rule sync. Old servers ignore unknown flag bits, so a new client against an old server degrades to single-transport return traffic. `SessionOutboundRouter` keeps one bounded map: the flow → transport value becomes `FlowReturn { transport, redundant }` (same 4096-entry bound, same eviction, same prune sites in `remove_transport` / `adopt_epoch` / `retire_by_peer`). `note_frame` records redundancy from the flag and REFRESHES it on every uplink data frame, so a live rule change takes effect on the flow's next uplink packet (an unflagged uplink clears the flow back to single-transport return); PING/PONG never mark a flow (the control guard runs first, whatever other bits the frame carries). `deliver`: a redundant flow in a multipath-capable session gets the SAME frame (same sequence — the client's copies are one frame cloned onto every transport, not re-sequenced) sent to EVERY live transport (clone per target except the last, which takes the original); a closed sender is pruned and the fan-out continues; the frame is dropped only when no live transport remains. Gated on `multipath_capable` like per-flow spreading — an old client that never advertised FLAG_MULTIPATH_RECV only reads its active transport, so copies elsewhere would be lost (and only a new client can set the flag at all). With one live transport the fan-out is exactly one copy. DEDUP + COST, STATED HONESTLY: there is NO new dedup layer anywhere — duplication is protocol-dependent. CLIENT RECEIVING SIDE: the Android worker writes every received frame's payload straight to TUN with no session-layer reorder or dedup (deliberate — forcing in-order delivery stalls inbound), so duplicate downlink packets reach the Android kernel twice: TCP dedups in-kernel by sequence number (the duplicate segment is dropped, extra ACKs only); UDP duplicates reach the app (the app sees the datagram twice). SERVER RECEIVING SIDE: the server has no SessionState — duplicate uplink packets are ingested twice into smoltcp, where smoltcp TCP dedups duplicate segments and duplicate UDP datagrams are delivered twice to the upstream socket. The ONE exception is the Linux client: its single shared `SessionState` drops the second copy while buffered (`or_insert`) or as stale after delivery (now benign, not fatal, per above). COST: N× bandwidth on every duplicated flow, BOTH directions — a 2-transport redundant stream costs 2× the bytes of a pinned one, and N× with N transports; the per-path byte counters show it. UI: `isImplemented` is now true for redundant in Rust AND Dart, so the fallback note ("Not live yet — falls back to Default until …") shows for NO mode any more — no screen text was hand-edited, it follows the model (one stale doc comment in `traffic_rules_screen.dart` updated; the screen test that asserted the note for `redundant` is now a no-note assertion). Tests: core `traffic_mode` (2 new: redundant order covers all transports regardless of hash/signals + single/empty degenerate cases, `mode_for_packet` through a redundant rule + unmatched/non-IP → default; `isImplemented` test now asserts all five) + FFI `redundant_fan_out_covers_healthy_prefix_only` (1 new: all-healthy, paused excluded, paused-first → empty, all-paused → empty, single → [0], empty → []) + server router (6 new: fan-out to all transports with identical sequence/payload + exactly one copy each, single-transport exactly one copy, unflagged uplink clears redundancy, no fan-out without multipath capability, dead sender pruned mid-fan-out with the survivor still served, PING+REDUNDANT does not mark a flow) + Dart `traffic_mode_test.dart` `isImplemented` expectations updated + `traffic_rules_screen_test.dart` fallback-note test replaced. Suites: flutter analyze clean, flutter test 117/117 (unchanged count — the screen test was rewritten, not added); core 86/0 (was 84/0; +2 new); server 113/4 (was 107/4; +6 new — the 4 are the same pre-existing sandbox-UDP failures, 2 server_integration + 2 wireguard, "Operation not permitted", same names as the 4.52 baseline); client 61/7 (identical 7 pre-existing QUIC-timeout names, stash-verified on the clean tree); ffi 24/1 (was 23/1; +1 new — the 1 is the same pre-existing rustls CryptoProvider panic in `android_session_runtime_can_pair_and_exchange_packets`); Kotlin untouched, not re-run. Sandbox note: UDP sending is blocked here, so QUIC runtime behavior is verified by code inspection + non-UDP tests only, as in prior slices. Open: live device checks (a `redundant` rule for `listen.mixlr.com` actually duplicating the stream on both paths; a UDP flow's duplicate datagrams reaching the app as documented; measured N× cost on a real session; return traffic fanning out on the deployed server); Dell server deploy for the return half (binary staged, needs a word — the 4.52 return half ALSO still awaits its deploy, same binary covers both); CLI rule input (file/flag); whether the 4.47 session-grade card stays (still open from 4.50). |
| 4.53 | Bandwidth boost mode (loss-weighted flow spreading) | Implemented + tested locally; UPLOADED Play internal v259 (status=completed, supersedes v253) 2026-11-06; NOT deployed (client-only, no server change — the 4.52 return half already covers it), NOT pushed | `order_candidates` gains a `bandwidth_boost` arm (`bandwidth_boost_order` + `bandwidth_weight` in `bonded-core` `traffic_mode.rs`): the loss-weighted refinement of the default spreading. WEIGHTING FORMULA: `weight = (grade / 100) * (1 - loss_rate)` — grade is the 4.50 per-transport total (higher = healthier), loss is the 4.49 QUIC loss rate (lower = healthier), clamped to [0, 1]; RTT deliberately does NOT enter the weight (latency is `lowest_latency`'s job — boost is about how much traffic a path can carry, not how fast it answers). The flow hash picks one transport with probability proportional to its weight — a deterministic function of (flow_hash, weights) via a `flow_hash % 1_000_000` slot scaled into [0, total weight), so a flow stays pinned to its first candidate while the transport set and the signals are stable, with no per-packet re-pinning, no per-packet striping, and no per-flow state in the engine (the same pinning discipline as 4.52). The remaining transports follow in weight order (ties by index) as the failover try-list. UNKNOWN SIGNALS are neutral, never perfect and never zero: unknown grade → 50 (midpoint of 0–100), unknown loss → 5% (a non-finite rate counts as unknown too) — so an unmeasured path weighs 0.475 and gets a middling share until its first grade push / loss observation, and with nothing measured anywhere every weight is 0.475 and the spread is uniform, i.e. boost with no data IS the default spreading. FULLY-LOSSY (loss >= 1) or zero-grade transports weigh exactly 0: they never lead a new flow, but they stay in the try-list last as failover — a dead-looking path is still better than dropping the flow when every other send fails. ALL-ZERO weights (every path fully lossy or zero-graded) fall back to the exact default hash spread so traffic still flows. No new signals were needed: grade arrives on the existing 1 s `nativeUpdatePathGrades` push (4.52) and loss from the existing QUIC counters (4.49) — the FFI `path_signals` builder, the Linux client loop (default engine, inert), and Kotlin are all untouched. RETURN HALF — NO SERVER CHANGE: the 4.52 `SessionOutboundRouter` is mode-agnostic — `note_frame` records flow → transport on every uplink data frame regardless of which client-side mode pinned the flow, and `deliver` prefers the recorded live transport — so a bandwidth-boost flow's uplink arrives on its weighted-picked transport and its return traffic comes back on that same transport. Verified by code inspection of `note_frame`/`deliver` in `smoltcp_forwarder.rs`; no server code touched, no server tests re-run. UI: `isImplemented` is now true for bandwidth boost in both Rust and Dart, so the fallback note ("Not live yet — falls back to Default until …") shows only for `redundant` (4.54), in both the rules screen pickers and the Settings summary — no screen text was hand-edited, it follows the model. Tests: Rust `traffic_mode` tests (9 new: weight formula incl. neutral unknowns + NaN loss + RTT-ignored, 2:1 and 4:2:1 spread distributions over 1000 spread flow hashes — 669/331 and ~573/287/140, deterministic bucket boundaries + failover weight order, loss discounting a high-grade path below a clean mid-grade one, all-unknown uniform spread, known-vs-unknown ~656/344, fully-lossy/zero-grade never lead but stay listed, all-zero fallback equals default order, empty-set/single-transport, `order_for_packet` through a boost rule) + Dart `traffic_mode_test.dart` `isImplemented` expectations updated (no new Dart tests). Suites: flutter analyze clean, flutter test 117/117 (unchanged); core 84/0 (was 75/0; +9 new); client 61/7 (identical pre-existing failures — same 7 QUIC-timeout names as the 4.52 baseline, sandbox-UDP; this slice touches no client code); server/FFI/Kotlin not touched, not re-run. Sandbox note: UDP sending is blocked here, so QUIC runtime behavior is verified by code inspection + non-UDP tests only, as in prior slices. Pre-existing warning (stash-verified on the clean tree): unused top-level `Ipv4Addr`/`Ipv6Addr` imports in `traffic_mode.rs` — not introduced by this slice, left alone. Open: live device checks (a boost flow's share tracking its weight as grades/loss change; a fully-lossy path receiving no new flows but serving as failover; return traffic on the weighted-picked transport); Dell server deploy for the 4.52 return half (still needs a word — 4.53 needs no deploy of its own); CLI rule input (file/flag); whether the 4.47 session-grade card stays (still open from 4.50). |
| 4.55 | Multi-address peer-share advertisement (advertise ALL non-cellular addresses; consumer falls through dead addresses) | Implemented + tested locally; UPLOADED Play internal v261 (status=completed, supersedes v259) 2026-11-07; NOT deployed (client-only, no server change), NOT pushed. Release: commit `1de1712` (count 141), versionCode 261, AAB 93,027,943 B SHA `8bbb9b78…`. | PROBLEM + USER DIRECTION (2026-11-07): a hotspot phone advertises only its CELLULAR IP — the old single-endpoint selection preferred a /24 match with a server-path bind, and on a hotspot the only server path is cellular — so a peer on the hotspot LAN cannot dial it (carrier NAT): one-way relay. Server evidence: a device authenticates via the 7a as provider; the 7a never authenticates via the other phone. User direction, followed verbatim: advertise ALL non-cellular addresses, not just the hotspot gateway IP, not a single best pick; the consumer tries them until one works. CORE (`bonded-core` `peer_share.rs`): `PeerShareAdvertisement` gains `endpoints: Vec<String>` (`#[serde(default)]`); `endpoint` is kept as the primary (first of the list) for old single-endpoint readers and the mDNS service record. `txt_records()` emits both `endpoint` and `endpoints` (comma-joined); `from_txt_record_map` parses `endpoints` and falls back to a one-element list holding `endpoint` for old advertisements. New `set_endpoints(ips, port)` (rebuilds both fields, primary first) and `endpoint_addrs()` (parses the list, falls back to `endpoint`, skips unparseable entries). MDNS (`peer_discovery.rs`): `service_info_from_advertisement` still uses `endpoint` (primary) for the ServiceInfo IP/port; `advertisement_from_resolved_service` also reads the `endpoints` TXT key. SELECTION (`peer_runtime.rs`): `resolve_advertise_ip` → `resolve_advertise_ips` (plural); explicit `peer_share_advertise_ip` pins a single address (unchanged); a pinned bind address likewise advertises only itself (the listener only serves it); otherwise ALL non-cellular, non-tunnel, bindable addresses, primary first (the existing path-subnet preference via `select_advertise_ip_for_binds`), rest in sorted order. CELLULAR EXCLUSION by interface name (case-insensitive prefixes): `rmnet*` (Android Qualcomm), `ccmni*` (MediaTek), `wwan*` (Linux), `pdp*` (older Android). TUNNEL EXCLUSION: name equals the configured `tun_name` (Linux `bonded0`), name starts with `tun` (Android VpnService creates tun0/tun1/…), or the address is a known client tunnel address (`10.8.0.2` / `fd00:10:8::2` — identical on every client, so a peer dialing it would reach its own tunnel). Filtering is factored into `advertisable_ips_from_pairs(&[(&str, IpAddr)], tun_name)` so tests use plain (name, IP) tuples; `advertisable_interface_ips` is the thin adapter over `&[NetworkInterface]`. The pure helpers (`prefer_path_subnet_candidate`, `select_advertise_ip`, `select_advertise_ip_for_binds`, `is_bindable_advertise_ip`) are unchanged with their tests. New dev-dependency `ipnetwork` 0.20 (test-only, to build `NetworkInterface` values in tests; already in Cargo.lock via pnet_datalink, no new download). LISTENER: `build_listener_runtime` now returns `Vec<IpAddr>` and no longer returns cert/key DER (they were only passed to the watcher for re-binding, which no longer exists); an unspecified bind listens on the wildcard of the primary's address family (`0.0.0.0` / `[::]`) so every advertised address is dialable; a pinned bind is unchanged. WATCHER: `run_advertise_watcher` rewritten — tracks `current_ips: Vec<IpAddr>`, recomputes `desired_advertise_ips` every 5 s, re-registers the mDNS advertisement with the same instance nonce on change; it NEVER re-binds, so existing legs are never disturbed by an interface change. `AcceptLoopSet`/`keep_accept_loop` removed (a single `AcceptLoop` Drop guard holds the one accept-loop handle; the prune test is removed with them). The watcher signature drops `identity`, `max_consumers`, `bandwidth_kbps`, `path_claims`, `active_relays`, `relay_stats`, `cert_der`, `key_der`. CONSUMER (`peer_listener.rs`): `connect_peer_relay` gains `endpoints: &[SocketAddr]` (last parameter) and tries each in order; fallthrough applies ONLY to the QUIC dial failing — once connected, the cert check, introduction send, and accept-response verdict are final for that leg (NoPath stops the leg loop, Rejected is an error). An empty list falls back to the introduction's `provider_endpoint`. The introduction binds the provider (key/nonce/cert), NOT the address — the provider's `verify_provider_introduction` never checks `provider_endpoint` and the server only requires it non-empty — so the same introduction authorizes a leg on any advertised address. The dial-loop call site passes `advertisement.endpoint_addrs()`. FAMILY-SCOPING LIMIT: the advertised list is scoped to the primary's address family — the listener binds that family's wildcard, so an address of the other family would not be dialable; a dual-stack provider advertises only its primary-family addresses. DEAD-ENDPOINT COST: each leg dial tries dead addresses in order; a refused address fails fast, a UDP-blackholed address costs a QUIC dial timeout per attempt, so a provider with many unreachable addresses slows leg establishment; the introduction is valid 300 s and the watcher refreshes the list every 5 s. BACKWARD COMPAT: old single-endpoint advertisements parse as one-element lists; new multi-endpoint advertisements on an old client read the first endpoint only (the primary) — acceptable. NO SERVER CHANGE. REVERSE DIAL (user's additional direction, evaluated, NOT included): reverse establishment — the provider initiating the relay leg when the consumer cannot dial — is a BIGGER protocol change, not a small addition. It would need: (1) signaling — the provider must learn the consumer wants a leg (server-mediated: the consumer's introduction request carries a "dial me back" flag + the consumer's endpoints and the server pushes that to the provider on its existing session, or the provider polls; or speculative dial-back on discovering a consumer's advertisement, which dials peers that may not want a leg and lets both sides dial simultaneously); (2) a reversed introduction — today's introduction is minted by the consumer's request and binds consumer→provider→provider_endpoint; the provider-as-dialer needs an introduction naming it as dialer and the consumer as listener (a new request direction or a symmetric introduction), and the consumer-as-listener must verify it (a mirror of `verify_provider_introduction`) plus gain an accept path that turns an incoming relay connection into a transport (today only the provider has `run_peer_accept_loop`/`accept_peer_relay`; the consumer only dials); (3) coordination/dedup — both sides initiating can create duplicate legs for the same provider path, needing a deterministic tie-break (e.g. only the lexicographically-lower device key initiates) or leg-tracker dedup by (peer, path) across directions. Cost: a full slice (or two) spanning server (new signaling message + routing, new introduction variant) and both clients (consumer-side accept path, dial coordination), with its own server deploy — a follow-up slice candidate, not this one. Why multi-address is the right primary fix: the observed hotspot failure is that the consumer cannot reach ANY advertised address because only the unreachable cellular one was advertised; multi-address fixes reachability directly with no protocol or server change. Reverse dial addresses a different, unobserved failure (a provider wholly unreachable from the consumer). Tests: new core tests (multi-endpoint round-trip, old-format parse, `set_endpoints`) + client `peer_runtime` tests (explicit-override plural form, pinned-bind single, cellular+tunnel exclusion, cellular name prefixes, tunnel name/address matching, all-non-cellular primary-first, error when only cellular+tunnel exist) + client `peer_listener` fallthrough test (dead `127.0.0.1:1` then the live listener → Accepted). Suites: flutter analyze clean, flutter test 117/117 (unchanged — no Dart touched); core 88/0 (was 86/0; +2 new); client 66 passed / 8 failed (was 61/7; the +6 new non-UDP tests all pass; the +1 new QUIC fallthrough test fails only on the sandbox UDP block — "QUIC connection failed: timed out", the same cause as the 2 baseline QUIC relay failures; the 7 baseline failures are identical by name); ffi 24/1 (baseline — the same pre-existing rustls CryptoProvider panic in `android_session_runtime_can_pair_and_exchange_packets`); server 112/5 vs stash-verified baseline 113/4 — the extra failure is the flaky smoltcp `dial_first_retransmitted_syn_dials_once` (passes in isolation; clean-HEAD full run is 113/4 with the same 4 pre-existing sandbox-UDP failures, 2 server_integration + 2 wireguard); Kotlin unit via `:app:testDebugUnitTest`: BUILD SUCCESSFUL (UP-TO-DATE — no Kotlin/Dart inputs changed since the 42/42 baseline recorded in 4.52, so Gradle correctly skipped re-execution; the bare `testDebugUnitTest` task fails compiling the `mobile_scanner` plugin's own test — pre-existing, documented in 4.51, unrelated to this slice). A forced re-run (`cleanTestDebugUnitTest` + `testDebugUnitTest`) was attempted but its single-use daemon died at fork with no task output while the box had only ~543 MB available under the concurrent Hawks 1.0.11 release build in another chat (which also holds the shared flutter_tools `buildOutputCleanup` lock per the AGENTS.md note — the first `:app:` attempt failed on that lock while the Hawks build held it; the wait-script run then succeeded once the first Hawks build finished). No Kotlin is touched by this slice, so the UP-TO-DATE verdict stands; re-run the `:app:` task for a fresh count if wanted after the Hawks build finishes. Sandbox note: UDP sending is blocked here, so QUIC runtime behavior is verified by code inspection + non-UDP tests only, as in prior slices. Pre-existing warnings (not introduced by this slice, left alone): unused top-level `Ipv4Addr`/`Ipv6Addr` imports in `traffic_mode.rs`, unused `Arc` import in `peer_listener.rs`, `mut upstreams` at `peer_runtime.rs` startup. Open: live device check on the 7a/8 Pro hotspot pair (does the 7a now relay via the 8 Pro's hotspot-LAN address?); reverse dial as a follow-up slice (design + cost above); peer relay renewal defect (relays die at the 300 s introduction expiry with no renewal — still open); whether the 4.47 session-grade card stays (still open from 4.50). |
| 4.56 | Reverse connection establishment: server signaling + reversed introduction (provider dials consumer when consumer cannot dial provider) | Implemented + tested locally; DEPLOYED to Dell as localhost/bonded-server:reversed 2026-11-07 (container up, health 200, binary SHA verified in-container), NOT pushed. Release: commit `669ef5f` (count 143), versionCode 264 (client v264 carries 4.56+4.57), Play internal v264 status=completed. | SIGNALLING (chosen: server-mediated push; alternatives considered and rejected: provider polling — a new poll loop + latency for an event the server already observes; speculative dial-back on discovering a consumer advertisement — dials peers that may not want a leg and lets both sides dial simultaneously, needing the dedup this slice deliberately defers; piggyback on heartbeat PONGs — true old-client invisibility but touches 3 PONG sites + a pending store + up-to-25 s idle latency, more moving parts than the direct push). When the server mints the forward introduction for a dial-back request it ALSO mints a reversed introduction and pushes a `PeerShareDialBackNotification` (consumer key + consumer endpoints + reversed introduction) to the provider's live session via the existing session router (`DialBackPush`, new `crates/bonded-server/src/dial_back.rs`: `SessionRegistry.session_id_for` → forwarder context → `SessionOutboundRouter::requeue`; wired into `BootstrapContext` in `main.rs`, `None` in tests). NO queueing: provider with no live session (no registry entry, or no forwarding context) → push dropped, returns false, introduction still mints; the consumer's next introduction request re-pushes. REVERSED INTRODUCTION: same POST endpoint, same 300 s expiry (`expires_at` shared by both mintings), `PeerShareIntroductionClaims` gains `direction: "forward"|"reverse"` (`#[serde(default)]` → "forward", so pre-4.56 claims parse as forward) + `consumer_endpoints: Vec<String>`; on a reversed introduction `listener_cert_fingerprint` is the CONSUMER's listener cert (the consumer is the listener) and `provider_endpoint` still names the provider's listener endpoint. The HTTP response still carries ONLY the forward introduction — the consumer never needs the reversed one (the provider presents it on dial and as its relay registration), so the response shape is unchanged for old clients. REPLAY: direction is part of the server-signed claims; `verify_provider_introduction` + `verify_consumer_introduction` now reject reversed introductions, and new pure verifiers `verify_reversed_introduction_for_dialer` (provider key + non-empty consumer key/endpoints/fingerprint + not expired) and `verify_reversed_introduction_for_listener` (consumer key == own listener identity key + provider key + own listener fingerprint + not expired) reject forward ones. SIGNED PAYLOAD: `PeerShareIntroductionRequest.signed_payload` covers ONLY the original six fields, deliberately NOT the new `dial_back` / `consumer_endpoints` / `consumer_listener_cert_fingerprint` fields — an old server verifies with its own six-field payload, so a new client signing an extended payload would fail against it. The dial-back fields ride inside the TLS bootstrap request; the server acts on them only after the core signature verifies; a flipped flag/endpoints cannot yield a usable leg because the provider's dial (4.57) verifies the server-signed reversed introduction's listener cert fingerprint first. A dial-back request with empty endpoints or empty consumer listener fingerprint is rejected 400 before any minting. CLIENT REQUEST SIDE (`peer_runtime.rs`): new `DialBackParams { consumer_endpoints, consumer_listener_cert_fingerprint }`; `build_peer_introduction_request` / `request_peer_share_introduction` take `Option<&DialBackParams>`; `start_peer_share_runtime` builds it from the local listener identity + own advertised endpoints when in provider mode (Both/ProviderOnly) and passes it into the browse task; consumer-only runtimes request no dial-back (no listener to dial). STALENESS: endpoints are the STARTUP advertised list; the advertise watcher may re-register a changed list later, so a list change between request and dial is a known window (next introduction cycle refreshes it). ACCOUNTING: `verify_relay_registration` (`peer_relay.rs`) accepts both directions (rejects only unknown directions) — direction governs the peer leg, not the server binding: the provider authenticated to the server either way and the session is bound to the consumer, so `relayed_via_provider` is identical for reverse legs; no accounting change needed. OLD-CLIENT PUSH BEHAVIOR — VERIFIED, NOT ASSUMED: the task's "old clients ignore unknown frame types" is FALSE. Read both inbound loops: the Android worker delivers every non-PONG frame's payload straight to TUN (the JSON is dropped by the kernel as a non-IP packet, counts one inbound packet) and the Linux loop feeds it to `ingest_inbound` (buffered past the reorder gap at seq u64::MAX, never delivered, never consumes a data sequence; a connection-id mismatch would be fatal, so the frame carries the session's own connection id). Neither errors nor kills the session; the notification is rare (one per dial-back request). A PONG-bit cloak was considered and REJECTED: both loops skip PONG payloads, but a PONG-flagged frame would write a bogus `last_rtt_ms` into path stats and corrupt transport grading. New `FLAG_PEER_DIAL_BACK` (0x0080, `session.rs`); frame header is session connection id + sequence u64::MAX. BACKWARD COMPAT: old request (no flag) behaves exactly as before (flag defaults off, reversed introduction only minted when requested, push is a no-op without a live session); new client vs old server degrades to consumer-initiated only (old server ignores the unknown JSON fields, mints forward only — signature still verifies because the signed payload is unchanged); old client vs new server keeps working (old requests mint forward only; the push never reaches an old client that never asks for dial-back). HOTSPOT WALK-THROUGH (7a/8 Pro): 8 Pro is the hotspot (its cellular IP is the only server-path bind, so 4.55 advertises its hotspot-LAN address too, but the 7a still cannot dial it — e.g. the hotspot LAN isolates clients or the 8 Pro's listener is not reachable from the LAN); 7a is on the hotspot Wi-Fi and runs a peer listener (provider mode) with its own advertised endpoints (its hotspot-LAN address 192.168.43.x + any other non-cellular address) and listener cert fingerprint. 7a discovers the 8 Pro's advertisement, requests an introduction with dial_back=true + its endpoints + its listener fingerprint; the server mints the forward introduction (7a keeps dialing on it as before) AND a reversed introduction naming the 7a as listener, and pushes the notification to the 8 Pro's live server session; the 8 Pro (4.57) dials the 7a's hotspot-LAN endpoint, presents the reversed introduction, the 7a verifies it as listener, and the 8 Pro opens a server session carrying the same introduction as its relay registration — the server binds that session to the 7a and marks it relayed_via_provider, so the 7a's traffic flows via the 8 Pro even though the 7a could never dial the 8 Pro. Tests: new core tests (dial-back fields excluded from signed payload + JSON round-trip, old request parses as forward-only, old claims parse as forward, reversed claims name roles/endpoints, notification control-frame round-trip + wrong-flag rejection) + client `peer_listener` tests (forward verifiers reject reversed, reversed verifiers accept matching + reject forward + wrong listener fingerprint + empty endpoints) + client `peer_runtime` tests (no-dial-back request is exactly the pre-4.56 shape with verifiable signature, dial-back request carries flag/endpoints/fingerprint + JSON round-trip) + server `dial_back` tests (push delivers to live transport with session connection id + seq MAX + parses as notification, drops without live session, drops without forwarding context, frame header) + server `bootstrap` tests (handler mints forward + pushes reversed with 300 s expiry + server signature verifies, drops push without provider session but still mints, 400 without endpoints/fingerprint) + server `peer_relay` tests (reversed registration binds with relayed_via_provider=true, unknown direction rejected). Suites: core 93/0 (was 88/0; +5 new); client 72 passed / 8 failed (was 66/8; +6 new non-UDP tests all pass; the 8 failures are the identical baseline set — 7 pre-existing + the 4.55 QUIC fallthrough test, all sandbox-UDP "QUIC connection failed: timed out"); ffi 24/1 (baseline — same pre-existing rustls CryptoProvider panic in `android_session_runtime_can_pair_and_exchange_packets`); server 122/4 (was 113/4; +9 new; the 4 are the same pre-existing sandbox-UDP failures, 2 server_integration + 2 wireguard). No Dart/Kotlin touched, so flutter/Kotlin suites not re-run (same standing as 4.54). No new warnings (all warnings in the workspace check are pre-existing). Sandbox note: UDP sending is blocked here, so QUIC runtime behavior is verified by code inspection + non-UDP tests only, as in prior slices. Open: 4.57 — consumer-side accept path (intercept FLAG_PEER_DIAL_BACK before the data path, verify with `verify_reversed_introduction_for_listener`, turn the incoming relay connection into a transport) + provider dial-back logic (on notification, verify with `verify_reversed_introduction_for_dialer`, dial consumer endpoints, present reversed introduction, open relayed server session) + dedup/tie-break for legs established in both directions (e.g. only the lexicographically-lower device key initiates, or leg-tracker dedup by (peer, path) across directions — NOT decided here); live device check on the 7a/8 Pro hotspot pair (does the reverse leg establish and carry the 7a's traffic via the 8 Pro?); Dell server deploy for the push + reversed minting (needs a word); peer relay renewal defect (relays die at the 300 s introduction expiry with no renewal — still open, reversed legs respect the same expiry); whether the 4.47 session-grade card stays (still open from 4.50). |
| 4.57 | Reverse connection establishment: client side — consumer accept path + provider dial-back + dedup (provider dials consumer when consumer cannot dial provider) | Implemented + tested locally; UPLOADED Play internal v264 (status=completed, supersedes v261) 2026-11-07; server half deployed to Dell as localhost/bonded-server:reversed (4.56 image), NOT pushed. Release: commit `f4a500a` (count 144), versionCode 264, AAB 93,337,155 B SHA `06649fe9…`. | CONSUMER ACCEPT (`peer_runtime.rs` `run_peer_accept_loop` + `peer_listener.rs` `accept_peer_relay_any` / `accept_peer_relay_reversed`): the accept loop now reads the introduction first and branches on direction instead of verifying forward-only. A reversed introduction verifies as listener against the consumer's OWN listener identity (consumer key == local device key, fingerprint == own listener cert; the expected provider is the one the server signed into the claims — the server signature is the trust root for which provider the consumer asked to be dialed back by, and the server only mints a reversed introduction for a provider this consumer requested dial-back for). The consumer claims no provider path and opens no upstream for a reversed leg — the transport is handed to the browse loop via a `ReversedLeg` channel (peer id = provider key + instance nonce from the claims) and becomes a session `ClientTransport::peer_relay` exactly like a forward leg this device dialed itself, with the same renewal notice and close re-drive. Provider-only runtimes reject reversed introductions (no consumer side to hand the leg to). PROVIDER DIAL-BACK (`run_dial_back_handler`, spawned in provider mode; notifications arrive via `PeerShareRuntime::dial_back_tx()`): for each pushed notification it plans (verify as dialer + parse consumer endpoints + claim a provider path), dials the consumer's endpoints in order with `connect_peer_relay_reversed` (shared `dial_peer_relay` core with the forward dial — same QUIC dial fallthrough, listener-cert pinning against the claims fingerprint, introduction presentation, accept-response verdict; NO fallback to `provider_endpoint`, which names the provider's own listener), and on accept opens the relayed server session on the claimed path with the shared `run_relay_task` (the forward accept loop's relay pump, extracted verbatim: upstream re-establishment pinned to the claimed path, relay registration with the reversed introduction, expiry abandonment, peer-closed detection, claim release, wakeup accounting). `no_path` from the consumer is the stop signal (consumer-side dedup refused the leg); a failed dial releases the claim and drops the notification — the consumer's next introduction request re-pushes, no retries here. INTERCEPT (both clients, before the data path): new shared `peer_runtime::forward_dial_back_frame` — a `FLAG_PEER_DIAL_BACK` frame is parsed as a `PeerShareDialBackNotification` and forwarded to the dial-back handler, never reaching the reorder buffer or TUN. Wired into the Linux packet loop (`run_linux_packet_loop`, new `dial_back_tx` param) and the Android FFI worker (local from the runtime) at BOTH receive points: the steady-state `recv_batch` arm (before the PONG check) and the session-binding PONG wait (a notification arriving mid-binding is forwarded and the binding ping re-sent, instead of failing session start on a non-PONG frame — the push can arrive any time after auth, since the session is registered then). The path-supervisor re-dial bind (`bind_redialed_transport`) is deliberately NOT wired: a dial-back frame there is a retryable bind failure (the supervisor re-dials; the consumer's next introduction cycle re-pushes) — plumbing a sender into the supervisor was out of scope for this slice. DEDUP — CHOSEN (b) LEG-TRACKER DEDUP BY (peer, path) ACROSS DIRECTIONS, NOT (a) TIE-BREAK: both directions stay available as fallback, which matters because in the hotspot case the forward direction is known-dead — a tie-break (only the lexicographically-lower key initiates) would strand the consumer if the designated initiator's direction is the broken one, and would need timeout/fallback complexity to recover. Dedup is enforced in two layers: (1) PROVIDER SIDE, authoritative for paths — `RelayPathClaims` is now shared by the forward accept loop AND the dial-back handler (hoisted into `start_peer_share_runtime`); `plan_dial_back` claims BEFORE dialing, so a forward and a reversed leg for the same consumer + same expiry can never hold the same provider path (single-path provider: the second plan is dropped; multi-path: the reversed leg claims a free path and is complementary, not a duplicate; a newer generation still takes a path over, per the existing takeover rule). (2) CONSUMER SIDE — `ConnectedPeers` (shared live leg set) + `note_reversed_leg`: the accept loop refuses a reversed leg for an already-connected peer with `no_path` BEFORE sending `ok`, so the provider never opens an upstream for a leg the consumer would drop; the browse loop re-checks on handoff and closes any leg that arrives anyway (the race where a forward leg lands between the accept check and the handoff). The consumer can only dedup by peer (it does not know which provider path a reversed leg is pinned to); the path-level guarantee lives on the provider. Consequence, documented: a reversed leg for a peer with an existing forward leg is dropped even when it would have added a path — forward dial-until-`no_path` already claims all paths when the provider is dialable, so the reverse leg is only needed when no forward leg exists. BACKWARD COMPAT: old clients keep working (never accept a reverse leg, never send dial-back requests); new clients vs old server degrade to consumer-initiated only (4.56); a reversed leg's frames are ordinary session frames once accepted, so the session worker needs no direction awareness. NO SERVER CHANGE (4.56's push + reversed minting + both-direction registration suffice). NO CHANGE to 4.55 multi-address advertisement or to the 300 s expiry / renewal behavior (reversed legs die at expiry like forward ones; renewal re-dials forward via `last_seen`, which fails in the hotspot case — the leg then reforms through the redial + re-push cycle, with a gap; reverse-aware renewal is future work, NOT this slice). HOTSPOT WALK-THROUGH (7a/8 Pro): 8 Pro is the hotspot (cellular is its only server-path bind; 4.55 advertises its hotspot-LAN address too, but the 7a still cannot dial it — LAN isolation / listener unreachable from the LAN). 7a is on the hotspot Wi-Fi, runs Both mode with its own listener (hotspot-LAN address 192.168.43.x + other non-cellular addresses) and listener cert fingerprint. 7a discovers the 8 Pro's advertisement and requests an introduction with dial_back=true + its endpoints + its listener fingerprint; the server mints the forward introduction (7a keeps its forward dial attempt, which fails against the undiallable address) AND a reversed introduction, and pushes the notification to the 8 Pro's live server session. The 8 Pro's session worker intercepts the `FLAG_PEER_DIAL_BACK` frame before its data path and forwards it to its dial-back handler, which verifies as dialer, claims its single provider path (cellular), dials the 7a's hotspot-LAN endpoint in order, presents the reversed introduction, and — on the 7a's `ok` — opens a relayed server session carrying the reversed introduction as its relay registration; the server binds that session to the 7a and marks it relayed_via_provider, so the 7a's traffic flows via the 8 Pro even though the 7a could never dial the 8 Pro. Existing direction keeps working: the 8 Pro uses the 7a as transport via its own consumer-initiated forward dial (unchanged code path — the forward accept/dial/relay code is shared, not replaced; the accept loop's forward branch is the same claim + `ok` + `run_relay_task` as before, and the 4.55 fallthrough dial is untouched). Tests: new client `peer_listener` tests (reversed QUIC leg end-to-end via `accept_peer_relay_any` + `connect_peer_relay_reversed` with frame exchange both ways; reversed dial rejects a forward introduction before any socket work; reversed dial never falls back to `provider_endpoint`) + client `peer_runtime` tests (`plan_dial_back`: single-path claim, duplicate dropped when the only path is held at the same expiry, second path claimed when the first is held, newer generation takes over, forward introduction rejected with no claim taken, unparseable endpoints rejected before claiming; `note_reversed_leg`: accepted when not connected, dropped when a forward leg exists, accepted again after the last leg closes; `forward_dial_back_frame`: pushed frame (session id + seq u64::MAX) forwarded and round-trips through the channel, data frame untouched, garbage-payload dial-back frame still intercepted). Suites: core 93/0 (unchanged — no core touched); client 84 passed / 9 failed (was 72/8; +12 new non-UDP tests all pass; the +1 failure is the new reversed QUIC test, which fails ONLY on the sandbox UDP block — "QUIC connection failed: timed out", the same cause as the 3 baseline peer_listener QUIC failures; the 8 baseline failures are identical by name); ffi 24/1 (baseline — same pre-existing rustls CryptoProvider panic in `android_session_runtime_can_pair_and_exchange_packets`); server 122/4 (unchanged — no server touched; same 4 pre-existing sandbox-UDP failures, 2 server_integration + 2 wireguard). No Dart/Kotlin touched, so flutter/Kotlin suites not re-run (same standing as 4.56). No new warnings (`cargo check` warning list stash-verified identical before/after). Sandbox note: UDP sending is blocked here, so QUIC runtime behavior is verified by code inspection + non-UDP tests only, as in prior slices. Open: live device check on the 7a/8 Pro hotspot pair (does the reverse leg establish and carry the 7a's traffic via the 8 Pro?); Dell server deploy for 4.56 + this slice (needs a word — server push + reversed minting must be live before any of this runs); peer relay renewal defect (relays die at the 300 s introduction expiry with no renewal — still open; reverse-aware renewal is the follow-up, so a reverse-only leg reforms via redial + re-push with a gap); whether the 4.47 session-grade card stays (still open from 4.50). |
| 4.58 | Client-only idle-battery fixes: QUIC bind-failure cooldown + peer-runtime idle pacing + session-monitor 60 s background tick | Implemented + tested locally; NOT uploaded, NOT deployed (client-only, no server change), NOT pushed. No release yet — working-tree diff for review. | TEST CASE (2026-11-09, bench Pixel 6a on vc265): VPN up, screen off, hotspot off, on a 500 mA hub port, the 6a drained ~3%/hr and hit 49% by 06:03 PST; VPN off restored charging (~8%/hr, 34% → 38% in ~30 min after the stop). Dell server logs prove the 6a's QUIC direct path failed to bind 88 times overnight ("failed to receive initial session frame", ~15-20/hr in the morning, tracking the WSS session re-authentications 1:1) and the server pushed 198 dial-back notifications to it.
