LIVE VERIFICATION (2026-11-17 ~14:53 PST) — PASS, FIX PROVEN: installed vc291 on the 6a ~14:30 PST, session started ~14:31, sampler verdict ~14:53 — bonded-vpn-outb 0.08% avg (max 0.40%) over 19.7 min vs 92–93% before; process 0.72% avg (max 7.2%); battery 75→76%, temp steady 34.6 °C; mDNS_daemon 7–30 threads. PLAY UPLOAD (2026-11-17 ~16:12 PST): release AAB built from clean worktree bonded-v291 at 75bc43f (count 171 → vc291; first build failed resolving the Android Gradle Plugin — stale egress CA-13 on a cold Gradle home, imported CA-14 fingerprint B8:91:95:EB… and rebuilt, 35.5 min Gradle task); AAB SHA-256 6c6c68ae…; uploaded to Play internal as v291, status=completed, superseding v289. |
| 4.79 | mDNS thread leak: shut the browser daemon down on drop | Implemented + tested locally 2026-11-17; NOT uploaded, NOT pushed | One mDNS_daemon thread leaked per peer-share runtime teardown (746-790 threads on the 6a, ~6% CPU). ROOT CAUSE: mdns-sd's ServiceDaemon has no Drop impl, and the browser daemon is a local of the detached drain task — when the runtime is dropped, the drain task's future is dropped unpolled, so Exit is never sent and the thread lives until process death. The advertiser escaped this only because PeerShareAdvertiser has a Drop impl that calls shutdown(). FIX (crates/bonded-client/src/peer_discovery.rs, Rust only): new DaemonGuard wraps ServiceDaemon and calls shutdown() on drop (silent by design — the safety net for unpolled drops; explicit call sites log their own errors). Used by the advertiser (replacing its Drop impl) and by the browser drain task, so a dropped drain task still queues Exit. Drop for PeerShareBrowser sends BrowserCmd::Shutdown. Shutdown failures in the drain task's Shutdown/Recreate arms are logged instead of swallowed. TESTS: bonded-client peer_discovery 5/5. No thread-count regression test: the daemon thread cannot reach its event loop in this sandbox (no *:5353), so a thread-exit assertion cannot pass here even with the fix. Open: verify on device — thread count must stay flat across session stops.  LIVE VERIFICATION (2026-11-18 ~13:07-13:20 PST) — PASS, FIX PROVEN: 6a updated vc291 → v299 (install -r of the universal APK from the v299 AAB, upload-key signed; versionCode 299 confirmed). vc291 baseline: 1,188 mDNS_daemon threads, 1,187 after stopping the session (leak persists across stops). v299 cycle 1: 2 during the session → 0 after stop. Cycle 2: 0 during → 0 after. Cycle 3: 2 during (session left running). The count stays at 0–2 across three stop/start cycles vs 1,187 on vc291. The DaemonGuard holds. Session left running on v299 (tun0 up, battery 77%). Logcat captured live (3.5 MB, ~/bonded-v299-logcat.txt on Dell) — it contains ZERO client-side h3/bootstrap/capability lines, so the h3-bootstrap diagnosis is still blocked on the client side: the client does not log the h3 handshake at INFO level. |

FIX (Kotlin): PairedServerStore.selectPreferredRecord prefers peer-share-capable records, then recency (explicit device id still wins outright); DebugVpnActivity and VpnControlReceiver resolve through AppSettingsStore.selectedRecord (UI selection, then the fixed fallback); peerShareEnabled (and the diagnostics flags) logged at session start in BondedVpnService and in the two headless starters. TESTS: 3 new in PairedServerStoreSelectionTest (peer-share beats recency, newest peer-share wins among peer-share records, explicit id still wins). SUITES: :app:testDebugUnitTest 57/0 (was 54/0; +3 new). |

SUITES: :app:testDebugUnitTest 57/0. |
| 4.82 | WG peer-cleanup lag: recreate the runtime when the registry key changes (server) | DEPLOYED to Dell as localhost/bonded-server:wg-repin 2026-11-20 ~18:40 PST + LIVE-VERIFIED (6a binds cleanly, no churn) | A quick VPN restart left the 6a unable to establish a session for ~9 min (2026-11-11). ROOT CAUSE: the server's WG runtime is pinned to the old source address + old key, and recreation is gated on session cleanup that nothing WG-side can trigger (UDP has no disconnect event; the session stays registered via live fallback transports). A restart always provisions a fresh keypair, so the old Tunn can never handshake the new one. FIX (crates/bonded-server/src/wireguard.rs, server only): store the WG peer key on WireGuardServerPeer at creation; in ensure_peer_runtime, discard the existing runtime when the registry's wg_public_key differs from the stored key, even while the session is still registered. TESTS: 1 new (stale_runtime_is_recreated_when_registry_key_changes — same device, fresh key, session still registered → new runtime, new session_id). Parts 2 (accept handshake from a new source) and 3 (idle reap + remove the map entry) and the client discovery-PING fallback are follow-ups, not in this slice. DEPLOYED 2026-11-20 ~18:40 PST as wg-repin (binary from HEAD 4bb7cc4, 16,578,400 B, SHA-256 f2eebbc8…; image db7a10c519f4; compose backup docker-compose.yml.bak-2026-11-20-wg-repin; rollback = idle-gate). LIVE-VERIFIED 2026-11-20 ~18:41–18:45 PST: the 6a restarted and bound cleanly on first try — server log shows 'discarding stale wireguard peer runtime: registry key changed (client restarted)' then 'wireguard peer runtime created session_id=4' then WG heartbeat PING→PONG; session_id=4 stable 4+ min, zero 'all transport paths failed' lines, mesh re-established (discovered OnePlus + 2 relay dials succeeded 18:43:16). The key-churn loop is BROKEN. Residual: the 6a still re-provisions WG keys ~2 per 60 s (per-path rotation); the server absorbs it via discard+recreate and the session holds — that is 4.82 working as designed, not a failure. |

| 4.85 | Bidirectional peer transports: a reversed leg is an additional leg, not a duplicate (Tom, 2026-11-18) | SHIPPED as v304 (Play internal, status=completed, 2026-11-18 ~21:52 PST) + pushed to GitHub (f040cc2..794761e). LIVE-VERIFIED 2026-11-18 ~22:06 PST on the bench pair (6a <-> OnePlus, both v304) — checklist item 20 checked off. | Tom's requirement (checklist item 20): if phone A has paths A1/A2 and phone B has paths B1/B2, then A must have peer transports for B1 and B2, and B must have peer transports for A1 and A2. Idle legs are fine (traffic mode may leave them idle) — the requirement is presence, not traffic. ROOT CAUSE (investigation `~/workspace/bonded-six-fixes/investigation-peer-bidirectional-2026-11-18.md` §4.4): the consumer refused an inbound reversed leg whenever it already held ANY leg for that peer (peer-level dedup, two layers — the accept loop's `connected_peers.is_connected` refusal and `note_reversed_leg` returning None). A reversed leg can therefore only ever be the FIRST leg for a peer; it can never be an additional leg alongside forward legs, so the second direction of a peer pair cannot grow once the first direction has a leg. The peer-level check is also redundant: the provider claimed a path for the dial via `plan_dial_back` before dialing, so a free path exists by construction, and the provider's per-path `RelayPathClaims` (shared by the forward accept loop and the dial-back handler) is the authority on duplicates — at most one leg per (peer, provider path) in either direction. FIX (crates/bonded-client/src/peer_runtime.rs, Rust only): (1) the accept loop no longer refuses a reversed leg for an already-connected peer — it accepts whenever the provider dialed it; (2) `note_reversed_leg` now always records the leg and returns the renewal generation (u64, no longer Option<u64>), and the browse loop books it as a `ClientTransport::peer_relay` exactly like a forward leg; (3) the now-unused `connected_peers` parameter was removed from `run_peer_accept_loop` and its caller. The renewal-pending mark is still set during reverse-aware renewal and cleared on leg arrival (make-before-break bookkeeping), but nothing reads it in production code anymore — it is vestigial until the renewal direction is revisited. TESTS: 3 updated — `reversed_leg_accepted_as_additional_leg_when_peer_already_connected` (was `reversed_leg_dropped_when_peer_already_connected`: a forward leg + a reversed leg now yields count 2, generation 2, reversed=true), `reversed_leg_accepted_as_renewal_replacement_while_connected` (a further reversed leg without a pending renewal is now an additional leg, count 2, generation 3, not a drop), and the two `.expect()`/`.is_some()` call sites updated for the u64 return. SUITES: `cargo test -p bonded-client peer_runtime` 55/55; full client suite 108/9 — the 9 verified by name as exactly the baseline sandbox-UDP set (3 h3_bootstrap + mixed_websocket_quic_failover + multipath_can_mix_websocket_and_naive_tcp + 4 peer_listener QUIC). FOLLOW-UPS (not in this slice): (a) renewal direction follows the NEWEST leg only (4.67) — with mixed forward+reversed legs for a peer, a reversed-newest renewal requests dial-back only and the forward legs expire; the closed-peer redial re-drives a forward dial after expiry, so the mesh recovers, but the renewal should eventually renew both directions; (b) the discovery dedup (`legs.is_connected` skip) still drops a second discovery for an already-connected peer, so if the initial dial-until-NoPath loop stops early on a transient failure, the missing leg is not retried until a leg closes or a renewal fires; (c) provider-side legs are still invisible on the provider's own Paths card (aggregate "Relayed for others" only) — moot once the full mesh establishes, since each phone is then a consumer for the other's paths. LIVE VERIFICATION (2026-11-18 ~22:01-22:06 PST, bench pair 6a <-> OnePlus, both v304; 6a has 1 path, OnePlus has 2): 6a — 2 forward relay dials succeeded 22:01:18 (OnePlus accepted on path 0 and path 1), then accepted a reversed relay from the OnePlus 22:05:19 during renewal; UI Peer connections card shows 'Peer YeZMPfbc · leg 1' (PeerRelay, 300 ms, loss 0.0%, down 2.48 MB) and 'leg 2' (343 ms, down 316 KB) — two peer transports for the OnePlus's 2 paths. OnePlus — discovered the 6a 22:06:31, forward dial FAILED (connection lost), then accepted a reversed relay from the 6a 22:06:31 + 'reversed relay leg received' — one peer transport for the 6a's 1 path, via the reversed direction, which is exactly the scenario 4.85 was built for (the reversed leg is the fallback that establishes the second direction when the forward dial can't). Both directions proven; 4.85 WORKS live. Caveat: the 6a's session failed for ~3 min after the v304 install (WG key pinning — server runtime pinned to the old key, 4.82 NOT deployed to Dell; client provisions a fresh keypair on every retry), then healed on its own 21:59:17 (bound to session 31). Both peer legs on the 6a show grade F·40 (idle-leg grade issue — Tom's grade-99 proposal pending, separate item). |
| 4.86 | Idle-leg grade 99 exploration prior (Tom, 2026-11-18) | SHIPPED as v306 (Play internal, status=completed, 2026-11-18 ~22:44 PST) + pushed to GitHub (794761e..9d5193c, fast-forward). | Tom's proposal (2026-11-18 ~21:37 PST, approved 22:09 PST): grade an idle leg with insufficient data as 99 — just below a known-perfect 100, above most known-worse — so BestTransport tries it and it gathers data. ROOT CAUSE (investigation-peer-bidirectional §2a): an idle leg gathers no loss/bandwidth data, so its thin measured total (RTT alone, e.g. a peer relay at 300 ms scoring ~50) stays low and BestTransport never picks it — it can never win traffic, so it never gathers data. FIX (Dart only — android/lib/models/transport_grade.dart + android/lib/widgets/transport_grade_detail.dart; no Rust/Kotlin): TransportGradeTracker._grade returns the prior when the transport has insufficient data — sentTotal < 100 (minLossSentPackets) AND activeIntervals < 10 (minBandwidthActiveIntervals) — i.e. essentially unmeasured. Total 99, letter A, isPrior=true; the sub-scores stay the honest measurements and the detail view explains the 99 ('Not enough data yet — graded 99 so this transport gets tried. The grade becomes measured once it carries traffic.'). The prior ends as soon as EITHER traffic threshold is met (≥ 100 packets sent, or ≥ 10 active windows), so a bad leg is bounded: it gets one flow's worth of traffic, then a real grade (and send-failure eviction if it cannot carry it). The rule applies to ANY transport with insufficient data, not just peer legs — a fresh direct path goes from 100 (thin latency-only total) to 99, a negligible change, while the idle peer leg goes from ~50 (D/F) to 99. The 99 flows to Rust through the existing PathGradesService push (gradeFor().total), so BestTransport ranks on it with no Rust change. TESTS: 5 new in transport_grade_test.dart (idle peer leg at 300 ms → 99 prior; zero-sample path → 99 prior; prior ends once traffic arrives, 99 → measured 70; bandwidth data alone ends the prior; measured grade is never a prior) + 2 new widget tests in transport_grade_detail_test.dart (prior note shown; measured grade shows no prior note); 1 existing test updated (latency-only total 100 → 99 prior). SUITES: flutter test 105/105 (was 98/98; +7 new); flutter analyze clean. | 4.87 | h3 bootstrap observer logging + accept-noise downgrade (checklist item 6, Tom) | DEPLOYED to Dell as wg-repin 2026-11-20 ~18:40 PST (server half); client half LIVE-VERIFIED 2026-11-19 | DIAGNOSIS (2026-11-19 ~00:30-01:00 PST, server-side): ~138 h3 bootstrap connections established per 30 min vs ~163 'HTTP/3 accept failed' WARNs per hour — ~1 failed accept per bootstrap connection. The client opens one fresh QUIC+h3 connection per bootstrap call (capabilities + provision = 2 per WG bootstrap), sends one request, then drops it (driver_task.abort() in request_bootstrap_json_h3); the server's h3_conn.accept() loop then errors instead of returning None. The client logs NOTHING on the h3 path (no log lines in request_bootstrap_json_h3 at any level) — that is why the live logcat capture during the v299 session had zero h3 lines. FIX (client, crates/bonded-client/src/lib.rs): INFO log on h3 bootstrap attempt + outcome (host, method, path, ok/err) so the 6a's observer logs show the handshake; (server, crates/bonded-server/src/quic.rs handle_h3_connection): count requests served per connection and downgrade the accept error to debug once >=1 request was served — a client going away after getting its answer is normal single-request bootstrap teardown, not a failure; zero-request accept failures stay WARN. Needs bench verification on the 6a (client INFO lines present; server accept-failed rate falls while bootstrap still succeeds). Server half needs a Dell deploy (separate word) to take effect. VERIFICATION 2026-11-19 ~01:10 PST: cargo test -p bonded-client 108 passed / 9 failed — exactly the baseline set by name (3 h3_bootstrap + mixed_websocket_quic_failover + multipath_can_mix_websocket_and_naive_tcp + 4 peer_listener QUIC, all sandbox-UDP); no regressions from the client logging. ROOT CAUSE FOUND: no `tracing` subscriber is installed on Android. `tracing_subscriber` is only initialized in bonded-cli and bonded-server; the Android library never installs one, so `bonded-client`'s `tracing::info!/warn!` hit the default no-op dispatcher and were discarded. The earlier assumption that 'Rust info! reaches logcat (heartbeat lines prove it)' was WRONG — the heartbeat lines come from the FFI crate's own `alog_*` macros (__android_log_write, tag BondedFFI), not from `tracing`. FIX (crates/bonded-ffi/src/lib.rs + Cargo.toml, 2026-11-19 ~01:15 PST): a minimal `LogcatSubscriber` implementing `tracing::Subscriber` (INFO and above; unique span IDs; event formatted as `{target}: {message} {fields}`) forwarding to `alog` with level-mapped priority, installed once in `JNI_OnLoad` via `set_global_default`. This makes ALL bonded-client tracing visible on device, not just h3. `cargo check -p bonded-ffi -p bonded-client` clean (host); Android-target check needs the NDK clang from the Flutter build env — the APK rebuild is the authoritative check. Rebuild + reinstall + re-capture pending. LIVE-VERIFIED 2026-11-19 ~01:20 PST (vc308 debug APK, upload-key-signed SHA-256 b9e7b549…, install -r on the 6a; session started, tun0 up, battery 71%): logcat now shows 40 'HTTP/3 bootstrap' lines, ZERO failed — every h3 bootstrap handshake visible: peer-share introduction POST (01:20:48, ~40 ms), capabilities GET + wireguard/peer POST for BOTH WG paths (01:20:57–58, ~25–30 ms each), second introduction POST (01:20:58). The LogcatSubscriber works: client tracing now reaches logcat as `BondedFFI: bonded_client: …`. Client half of 4.87 COMPLETE. | 4.88 | WG peer-cleanup part 2 (roaming re-pin) + part 3 (idle reap); discovery-PING fallback (checklist item 13) | DEPLOYED to Dell as wg-repin 2026-11-20 ~18:40 PST; re-pin + reap live observation still open | Part 1 (85945fa) already shipped: runtime recreated when the registry key changes. PART 2 ROOT CAUSE (crates/bonded-server/src/wireguard.rs try_accept_datagram): the runtime pins peer_addr on first datagram and returns None for any other source BEFORE decapsulation — a client whose source address changes (NAT rebinding, wifi/cellular switch) with the same key can never handshake again; the datagram is dropped and no runtime discard triggers (key unchanged, session still registered). FIX: on pin mismatch, trial-decapsulate; on success (cryptographically valid WG packet — only the key holder can produce one) re-pin peer_addr to the new source and log it; on failure keep the old pin. Safe against the probe loop: TunnResult::Err => Ok(false), so a wrong-peer's packet consumes nothing. PART 3 ROOT CAUSE: runtimes map entries + sessions + forwarders are never reaped — cleanup_wireguard_peer runs only when a per-peer task's channels close or a send fails, and nothing closes them on idle; the map entry itself is never removed even by cleanup. FIX: track last_datagram_at per runtime; a periodic reap in run_wireguard_server (select! interval tick) removes entries idle > WG_IDLE_REAP_AFTER (5x the 60 s keepalive) via cleanup_wireguard_peer + map removal, as a pure reap_idle_wireguard_runtimes() fn testable with a fake now. DISCOVERY-PING FALLBACK: scope TBD during implementation — no TODO in code; decide from the client's WG-failure path whether it is a client retry (PING discovery to re-find the path) or a server pre-reap liveness check; if it does not resolve to a small well-defined change, record it as a scoped follow-up, not a silent omission. Server-side only; Dell deploy is a separate word. IMPLEMENTED 2026-11-19 ~01:20 PST: part 2 trial-decapsulate re-pin in try_accept_datagram (warn log on re-pin); part 3 last_datagram_at on the transport state + reap_idle_wireguard_runtimes (WG_IDLE_REAP_AFTER 300 s, 60 s interval tick via tokio::select! in run_wireguard_server) + tests wireguard_runtime_repin_accepts_same_peer_from_new_source and reap_idle_wireguard_runtimes_removes_stale_entries; cargo check -p bonded-server --all-targets clean. Server suite running to verify. DISCOVERY-PING FALLBACK — RESOLVED 2026-11-19 ~01:45 PST, NO CODE CHANGE: the fallback already exists in the path supervisor. A WG path dropped at session-start discovery (handshake timeout → PING send fails → path removed) is re-dialed 5 s after the supervisor spawns (INITIAL_MISSING_DELAY, path_supervisor.rs) and bound with a discovery PING/PONG (bind_redialed_transport, 10 s BIND_TIMEOUT). An immediate retry inside discovery was considered and rejected: it would delay session start by up to ~15 s whenever WG is truly dead, for 5 s of benefit. What remains is live verification (a session where WG fails at discovery, then the supervisor's re-dial + bind in the logs) — a bench check, not a build. SERVER SUITE 2026-11-19 ~01:35 PST: 130 passed / 5 failed. The new reap test PASSES. The new re-pin test fails — but so do the two PRE-EXISTING WG tests (wireguard_peer_runtime_exchanges_frames, wireguard_bootstrap_provisions_and_dials_transport) with the IDENTICAL error: try_accept_datagram's send_to gets EPERM (Operation not permitted) on this VM; Python cannot even create a UDP socket here. The failure is the sandbox, not the change — the pre-existing test fails on code the re-pin edit does not touch for a first datagram. The 2 server_integration relay failures are the same UDP/ICMP class. True verification of the re-pin test moves to Anvil (where UDP works); local result stands at compile-clean + reap-test-pass. The single failure is the ICMP echo relay (raw-socket timeout on Anvil, unrelated). Parts 2+3 fully verified; remaining: local commit + Dell deploy (separate word) + live verification. NEW FINDING (bench, 2026-11-20 ~17:00–17:11): the 6a fell into a WG key-churn session-recovery loop — "all transport paths failed session discovery" (WireGuard handshake timed out after 5 s) → recovery → fresh WG keypair → server runtime pinned to the old key (4.82, NOT deployed) → handshake fails again. Server log shows a new WG keypair for the 6a every ~7 s and "failed to bind QUIC session … failed to receive initial session frame". A 3-min quiet period (VPN_STOP) did NOT break it; the 6a's VPN is now stopped for a 10-min quiet to let the server WG runtime expire, then it will be restarted and re-checked. This is the known 4.82 failure mode, triggered here by the force-stop/cold-start giving the 6a a new keypair while the server was pinned to the old one; the Dell deploy of 4.82 (+ 4.87 server half + 4.88) would fix it server-side. UPDATE 2026-11-20 ~17:24 PST: the 10-min quiet did NOT break the loop — on restart the 6a immediately resumed churning (fresh WG keypair every ~7–10 s, "all transport paths failed session discovery", WG handshake timeout 5 s). The server's WG runtime for the 6a is NOT being discarded on its own within 10 min, so the loop is self-sustaining: each recovery provisions a new keypair and the server stays pinned to an old one. The 6a's VPN has been STOPPED to halt the churn (tun0 down, battery 71%); the OnePlus is left RUNNING and stable (tun0 up, 100%). Recovery now needs the server-side fix: the Dell deploy of 4.82 (+ 4.87 server half + 4.88), which discards the stale WG runtime on key change and adds the 300 s idle reap — that deploy still needs Tom's separate word. Do NOT restart the 6a's VPN until that deploy lands (or the server runtime expires on its own, unknown duration). The 6a stays down until the Dell deploy (4.82 discards the runtime on key change; 4.88 adds the 300 s idle reap) or a server restart / manual peer-registry clear. The 7a's dial-back to the 8 Pro FAILS on both advertised endpoints (10.112.188.237 cellular/CGNAT + 192.168.1.238 Wi-Fi, same LAN as the 7a's 192.168.1.230) — the 8 Pro's listener is unreachable and its mDNS advertisement never arrives, while its dial-OUT works (it dials the 7a forward fine). So the only working direction is the 8 Pro dialing out; the 7a dialing in always fails. The server never pushes a dial-back to the 8 Pro for the 7a (0 in 2 h) because the 7a never requests an introduction for the 8 Pro (it never discovers it), so no reverse leg is ever initiated. Root cause: peer discovery is mDNS-only, and the 8 Pro's advertisement never reaches the 7a (doze / hotspot interface binding suspected — not verifiable from the server side, and the 8 Pro uploads no diagnostics). FIX (mutual introduction): when a consumer requests an introduction with dial_back=true, the server ALSO mints a second reversed introduction with the roles swapped (consumer=original provider, provider=original consumer) and pushes a dial-back notification to the ORIGINAL consumer (as provider) for the ORIGINAL provider (as consumer). The 8 Pro then dials the 7a (the direction that works — it already dials the 7a forward), and the 7a accepts the reversed leg (4.57 accept path already handles it, including when it already holds a leg for the peer). Requires the server to know the 8 Pro's instance nonce for the mutual intro's provider_instance_nonce (used to form the peer_id on accept; wrong nonce would break renewal tracking), so the client sends `consumer_instance_nonce` in the introduction request (new serde-default field; old clients omit it and the server skips the mutual push, keeping old behavior). Client: DialBackParams gains the nonce from the listener identity; server: mint + push the mutual notification in bootstrap.rs. The 7a needs NO client change (it only accepts); the 8 Pro needs the new build (to send its nonce); the server needs the new code (deploy to Dell, standing rule). ## Phase 6: Unified Bootstrap Port + QUIC/HTTP3 + WireGuard

Goal: use the bootstrap port (taken from `server_public_address` in the pairing QR payload) as the single entry point for transport negotiation. WSS (TCP) and HTTP/3 (UDP) share the same host/port. WireGuard needs its own UDP port because QUIC and WireGuard packets cannot be demultiplexed on the same socket — but the client doesn't need to know it at pairing time; the bootstrap API hands it over after authentication. 443 is the conventional default for the bootstrap port, but any port works.

### Phase 6.0: Bootstrap and Edge Design Lock

| # | Task | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 6.0.1 | Define unified bootstrap API contract | not-started | Add versioned bootstrap endpoints under `/v1/bootstrap/*` for capability exchange and transport credential provisioning; usable over HTTPS (HTTP/1.1), WSS, and HTTP/3 |
| 6.0.2 | Define transport capability document | not-started | Server returns transport inventory (`wss`, `h3`, `wireguard`), endpoint host/port/proto, TTL, and retry/backoff hints |
| 6.0.3 | Define bootstrap token model | not-started | Pairing invite redemption issues a short-lived bootstrap token bound to device public key and server nonce; token authorizes transport-specific provisioning |
| 6.0.4 | Define parallel transport dial and preferred-path policy | not-started | All enabled transports dial concurrently (WSS, H3, WireGuard); the first to succeed on each interface is used immediately; a configurable preference order (e.g. `wss > h3 > wireguard`) controls which path is promoted to primary when multiple succeed; slower paths are kept as failover or dropped depending on scheduler policy; `naive_tcp` is a debug-only fallback not included in production dial set; bootstrap port always from `server_public_address` |
| 6.0.5 | Define TLS cert trust via ed25519 signature | completed | If TLS cert does not validate against system root CAs (self-signed or private CA), the client must open the TLS connection with certificate verification **disabled** for that one initial request, call `GET /v1/bootstrap/cert-proof`, then verify the ed25519 signature synchronously before exchanging any credential material — only after the signature validates does the client pin the cert and proceed; connecting with verify=false is safe only because the ed25519 check is the real trust anchor and happens before any secret is sent; client must abort immediately if the signature is invalid (MITM); when a system-trusted cert is present (e.g. Let's Encrypt), TLS verifies normally and this step is skipped; pinned cert fingerprint is persisted in `PairedServerStore` (Android) or client config file (Linux); if TLS fails on a future connection due to cert rotation, the client re-runs the cert-proof step rather than hard-failing; **SERVER SIDE IMPLEMENTED** — `GET /v1/bootstrap/cert-proof` endpoint live in `bootstrap.rs`; returns `{cert_fingerprint: "sha256:<hex>", ed25519_signature: "<base64>"}` signed by persistent server identity key |
| 6.0.6 | Define fd-protect API for all transport types | not-started | `ClientConfig` already carries a `SocketProtectFn` used by NaiveTCP; extend the same callback contract to cover UDP sockets created by QUIC (`quinn`) and WireGuard transports; on Android, every socket (TCP or UDP) must call `VpnService.protect(fd)` before `connect()`/`bind()` or it will be captured by the VPN TUN and deadlock; the protect callback must be invoked in each transport's socket-creation path before the socket is used; on non-Android platforms the callback is a no-op |
| 6.0.7 | Define TUN MTU budget and per-transport frame size limits | completed | Each transport adds overhead on top of the raw IP packet from the TUN: WSS ≈ 55 bytes (TLS record + WS frame + session frame header), QUIC/H3 ≈ 60 bytes, WireGuard ≈ 60 bytes (32-byte WG header + outer UDP/IP); if TUN MTU is 1500 and the outer network MTU is also 1500, there is no room for transport overhead and packets are fragmented or silently dropped; Android `Builder.setMtu()` is called once at VPN bring-up before transport paths are known so it must be set conservatively for the worst-case transport; default safe value is **1420** (matches WireGuard standard: `1500 - 80`), which provides headroom for all transports; each `ClientTransport` implementation must enforce a matching max session-frame payload size so the session layer never hands a transport a frame that will exceed the outer MTU after encapsulation; **IMPLEMENTED** — `bonded-core/src/config.rs` `tun_mtu` default changed to 1420; `BondedVpnService.kt` `setMtu(1420)` updated to match |
| 6.0.8 | Define NaiveTCP production retirement | completed | Session 59 retires NaiveTCP from production dial defaults. Shared client config now defaults to `wss`, `h3`, and `wireguard`; `establish_transport_paths()` filters `naive_tcp` out unless `allow_insecure_debug_transports` is explicitly enabled; and the Android FFI default session config now uses only secure transports while treating an explicit incoming `naive_tcp` protocol list as a deliberate debug opt-in. NaiveTCP remains available for targeted debug flows and tests, but it is no longer present in the normal production dial set. |

### Phase 6.3: WireGuard Transport (Bootstrapped over configured port)

| # | Task | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 6.3.1 | Select WG implementation strategy | completed | Use `boringtun` purely as a **crypto library**, not as a VPN. The inner payload of every WireGuard packet is a Bonded session frame — not a raw IP packet. There is no tunnel IP, no allowed-IPs list, no IP routing, and no second TUN. `boringtun` handles the Noise handshake, ChaCha20-Poly1305 encryption, and UDP framing; Bonded owns the socket, calls `boringtun::encapsulate(session_frame)` to get an encrypted UDP datagram to send, and calls `boringtun::decapsulate(udp_payload)` to recover the session frame on receipt. The session layer and scheduler see this as just another `ClientTransport` — identical in interface to NaiveTCP or WSS. The OS VPN TUN is owned exclusively by `BondedVpnService`; boringtun never touches it. This model deliberately gives up the "two peers forward raw IP" simplicity of standard WireGuard in order to preserve Bonded's multi-path bonding (CR-1/CR-2) — a WG-as-real-VPN path would be a completely separate tunnel with no session layer, making it impossible for the scheduler to bond it with WSS/H3 paths. Kernel WireGuard is deferred indefinitely as it is incompatible with this model on Android. |
| 6.3.2 | Define WG bootstrap payload | completed | `POST /v1/bootstrap/wireguard/peer` in `bootstrap.rs`: accepts `{device_public_key, wg_public_key}`, returns `{server_wg_public_key, peer_ip}`; `GET /v1/bootstrap/capabilities` includes `server_public_key` + `provision_endpoint` when WG enabled; `WireGuardPeerRegistry` in `bonded-server/src/wireguard.rs` allocates 100.64.1.x/32 IPs; server WG keypair persisted via `load_or_generate_wg_keypair()` |
| 6.3.3 | Add WG key lifecycle and peer state persistence | completed | Session 58 added persisted server-side WireGuard peer state backed by a TOML file (`wireguard_peers_file`, default `/var/lib/bonded/wireguard_peers.toml`) plus configurable lease duration (`wireguard_peer_lease_secs`). `WireGuardPeerRegistry::load_with_lease()` now reloads peer allocations on startup, prunes expired peers, normalizes legacy persisted entries, and safely reuses freed `/32` assignments without colliding with active peers. `register_peer_lease()` refreshes the lease on re-provisioning, rotates an existing device's WG public key while preserving its assigned `/32`, and exposes `lease_expires_at` through the bootstrap API so the client can reason about credential lifetime. Focused tests now cover persistence, expiry pruning, safe IP reuse, env overrides, and end-to-end bootstrap provisioning. |
| 6.3.4 | Implement WG transport adapter | completed | `WireGuardTransport` implements `Transport` trait in `bonded-core`; `establish_wireguard_session()` in `bonded-client`; `ClientTransport::WireGuard` variant; `("wireguard" | "wg")` arm in `establish_transport_paths`; client runtime now fetches `/v1/bootstrap/capabilities`, registers its WG public key via `POST /v1/bootstrap/wireguard/peer`, and dials the advertised WG UDP endpoint before returning a `WireGuard` path |
| 6.3.5 | Add WG revocation and teardown | not-started | Device revoke must invalidate WG peer immediately and terminate existing WG-backed paths |
| 6.3.6 | Add WG integration tests | completed | Added focused server/runtime test `wireguard_peer_runtime_exchanges_frames` proving encrypted frame exchange between the new server UDP runtime and `WireGuardTransport`, plus end-to-end bootstrap test `wireguard_bootstrap_provisions_and_dials_transport` proving the client fetches capabilities, provisions its WG peer over HTTP bootstrap, establishes a WG transport, and exchanges a frame; mixed-path failover coverage with WSS/QUIC still belongs to later scheduler-focused work |

### Bootstrap Sequence (Target)

1. Client pairs (QR or manual) and gets `server_public_address` (`host:port`), invite token, and server public key (ed25519).
2. The `host:port` from pairing **is** the bootstrap endpoint — no additional port needed.
3. Client opens TLS connection to `https://<host>:<port>`. If the TLS cert validates via the system root CA store (or a stored fingerprint pin), proceed normally. If it does not (self-signed, private CA, or no stored pin yet), the client connects with TLS cert verification **disabled**, immediately calls `GET /v1/bootstrap/cert-proof`, and verifies the ed25519 signature over the cert fingerprint using the server public key from the QR payload — no credential material is sent before this check; if the signature is invalid the connection is aborted (MITM); if valid the fingerprint is pinned and persisted. If TLS fails on a future reconnect due to cert rotation, the client re-runs this step rather than hard-failing.
4. Client proves device identity with device keypair over the now-trusted TLS channel.
5. Server returns a capability document: which transports are enabled, the HTTP/3 UDP endpoint (same host:port as bootstrap but UDP), and the WireGuard UDP endpoint (separate dedicated UDP port).
6. **Wave 1 — concurrent dial:** Client dials WSS and H3 concurrently across each available network interface. The first to succeed on each interface is used immediately. Preference order (WSS > H3 by default) promotes one to primary when both succeed on the same interface; the other is kept as hot standby. `naive_tcp` is a debug-only fallback excluded from the production dial set.
7. **Wave 2 — WireGuard provisioning (background):** After wave 1 establishes at least one working path, client requests WG peer config over the bootstrap API (requires a wave-1 path to be live). Server returns a signed peer assignment including the dedicated WG UDP endpoint, client tunnel IP, allowed IPs, and expiry. Client establishes WG path in the background and adds it to the session once live. WG cannot participate in wave 1 because it requires this bootstrap round-trip first.
8. Session layer uses all successful paths concurrently; scheduler remains transport-agnostic.

Acceptance gate:

- Single `host:port` from pairing payload reaches HTTPS/WSS (TCP) and HTTP/3 (UDP) on the same port number
- Bootstrap API is reachable via HTTPS/WSS and HTTP/3
- Self-signed cert is accepted after ed25519 cert-proof verification; fingerprint is persisted and reused on reconnect; cert rotation triggers re-proof rather than hard failure
- QUIC/HTTP3 path can carry session traffic and coexist with WSS in one session
- WireGuard path can be provisioned from bootstrap API (wave 2) and coexist with WSS/QUIC
- WireGuard peer assignments survive server restart without requiring re-pairing
- NaiveTCP is disabled in production server config; WSS is the default encrypted transport
- TUN MTU is set to 1420 (or lower); all transports enforce matching max frame payload sizes
- Optional Let’s Encrypt mode can issue and rotate certs without manual restart

## Phase 7: Peer Connection Sharing

CR-7 is no longer deferred. Base transports and bootstrap trust are in place, so the remaining work is the peer-sharing subsystem itself.

Initial local peer transport is QUIC (`bonded-peer` ALPN) carrying relayed `SessionFrame` bytes. |

Additional no-VPN explicit-bind probes now show the same timeout even when the listener is bound directly to `192.168.1.250` and the sender is bound directly to `192.168.1.140`, so the remaining work is no longer in the peer runtime itself; it is either LAN isolation or some lower-level device-to-device reachability issue on the current network. |
