Session 63 also fixed Android session snapshot bookkeeping so provider-side relays contribute to `peerRelayCount`; after upstream registration the OnePlus now reports `peerRelayCount=1` instead of incorrectly remaining at `0`. |

### October 2026 — production server + physical-device validation (builds 130–132)

The October server (post-auth session binding via an initial session frame, stricter TLS bootstrap listener) exposed three Android FFI defects that the May server had masked. All three were found on physical devices against the production server (dell, `charter.codingwell.net:443`) and fixed in sequence:

- **Deep-link pairing**: the server startup log emits a clickable `bonded://pair?...` deep link alongside the QR code; the Android app handles it via an intent-filter + app_links. Pairing was exercised headlessly over ADB (Pixel 6a via deep link, OnePlus One via `MainActivity adb_action=pair`) and in the UI.
- **Build 130 — FFI session-discovery fix**: the October server's `resolve_session_binding` waits for an initial session frame to detect peer-relay registrations. The Android FFI session worker authenticated and then sent nothing until the 25s heartbeat, so the server logged `failed to bind websocket session after authentication … failed to receive initial session frame` and dropped every path; no Android session ever bound. The FFI worker now performs the same startup PING/PONG session-discovery exchange as the Linux client on every path, adopts the server-assigned session id from the PONG (replacing the hardcoded `SessionState::new(1)`), and drops paths that fail the exchange. The Settings screen also now shows the Play build number via `package_info_plus`.
- **Build 131 — redeem transport fix**: `redeem_invite_token` always dialed the plaintext naive-TCP handshake, including to the TLS bootstrap endpoint (server logged TLS `InvalidContentType`; the app showed `unexpected end of file`), so pairing against production never worked. Redeem now dials via `establish_transport_paths` and runs the session-binding exchange over that transport (`complete_pairing_session_binding`). With 131, the first successful physical Android end-to-end validation completed: both phones paired and their VPNs connected to the October server, with traffic through the tunnel verified.
- **Build 132 — heartbeat + peer advertise fixes**: (a) the FFI heartbeat pinged only the active path, so the server's 30s receive timeout kept killing secondary paths; the heartbeat now pings every path. (b) Peer advertise-IP selection picked the VPN tunnel address `10.8.0.2` (identical on every client) over the LAN address; selection now prefers a candidate sharing a /24 with a path bind address.
- **Physical-device validation status (2026-10-17, build 132)**: both phones (Pixel 6a, OnePlus One) run build 132 against the production October server. Each binds two direct WSS paths (Wi-Fi + cellular) to one server session; over a 5½-minute observation window the server logged zero session teardowns or path drops (the pre-132 secondary-path timeout is gone), and heartbeats pong on both phones every 25s. The Pixel's peer runtime advertises `192.168.1.140:54443` (LAN, not `10.8.0.2`). A physical peer relay formed on the home WLAN itself: the Pixel discovered the OnePlus, issued an introduction, and its relay dial succeeded; the OnePlus accepted the relay, and the server added the relay transport to the Pixel's session with `relayed_via_provider=true` (3 transports total). Still open: forced direct-path-loss failover carrying user traffic on the phones (covered for the Linux client by the Forge netns harness), and Play upload of builds 131/132 — build 130 shipped to the Play internal track on 2026-10-16, but 131 was sideloaded only and 132 has not been uploaded; both await explicit user confirmation per the standing rule.
- **Forge netns harness regression (2026-10-17, build-132 code)**: the remote-peer harness (provider + consumer in Forge network namespaces, paired to the production dell server) passed **7/7** — peer control plane, 40s idle soak, and the forced direct-path-loss data plane (HTTP 200 via the provider relay) all green, so the 130–132 client changes did not regress the QUIC/UDP peer data plane. A first attempt failed at consumer startup on a transient path-establishment timeout (no server-side trace; the provider was unaffected) and the immediate retry passed in full. The run also confirmed the server closes idle relay-upstream transports at its 30s receive timeout and the provider re-registers each time; on the phones, the same cycle exposed a longevity gap — after the server closed the OnePlus's relay upstream at ~00:30 PDT, its re-registration retries reused the by-then-expired introduction and were rejected (`peer relay introduction expired`, 44 attempts) until the Pixel issued a fresh introduction at 00:45:48 and the relay re-formed (server transport re-added with `relayed_via_provider=true`). Follow-up: a provider whose relay upstream drops after its introduction has expired cannot recover until the consumer happens to re-issue; consider provider-triggered introduction refresh.
- **Client session-recovery downlink wedge — TUN-fd re-handoff fix (build 142; 2026-10-18)**: after any server restart, phones auto-recovered into a session that reported connected but passed no user traffic until a manual VPN toggle; reproduced 6/6 server bounces on both bench phones and on both the pre-fix and NAT-fixed server binaries (so it is a client defect, independent of the server). Root cause: the native TUN-fd direct-write handoff happens once at initial start, but the Rust side cleared the global TUN writer on every session stop (`stop_android_session` called `set_android_tun_fd(-1)`, and start calls stop first), while the Kotlin recovery/rebind paths restarted the native session without re-running `configureNativeTunFd` — the restarted worker queued every inbound frame into a per-session queue that nothing drains when the initial handoff succeeded. Fix: `stop_android_session` no longer clears the TUN writer (it is service-lifetime state owned by Kotlin's explicit `nativeSetTunFd` calls: set when the packet-IO loop starts, cleared by `stopPacketIoLoop` on full shutdown), and `BondedVpnService` re-runs the handoff after every successful session restart via `rehandoffTunFdAfterSessionRestart()` in both the recovery and network-rebind paths, falling back to the inbound drain thread (extracted as `startInboundDrainThreadIfNeeded`) if the handoff fails. Full analysis: goal checkpoint `session-recovery-wedge-2026-10-18.md`.
- **Server forwarder NAT fix — full-tuple flow keying (implemented 2026-10-18; server-side only, no client build)**: root-caused the user's report that the VPN was "really unreliable" in the FAST.com app on a local connection, with ~2× bandwidth. The server's smoltcp TCP forwarder proxied every flow behind one smoltcp identity with **one listener per destination port**, keying flow records by the reduced tuple (client IP, client port, dst port) and recovering the destination IP by lookup; two flows sharing that reduced tuple with different destination IPs (ordinary ephemeral-port reuse + a rotating destination) made every outbound packet for both get dropped fail-closed as an "ambiguous tuple", and new flows were rejected at promotion. Flow records also lingered long past death (zero-packet corpses ~1h), keeping the trap armed. FAST is the perfect trigger: dozens of parallel flows to many Netflix OCA IPs on :443 with aggressive retries — its flows died at TLS-handshake scale (~10–16 KB) while ordinary browsing mostly dodged the lottery. Production proof on the user's own session the same evening: 700+ dropped packets on one tuple, 14 drop warnings on :443 tuples, 1 rejected AWS :443 flow. Fix (`crates/bonded-server/src/smoltcp_forwarder.rs`): the forwarder is now a proper NAT — dispatch keeps a shared flow map keyed by the full 4-tuple, allocates a **unique mapped port per flow** (49152–65535) on SYN (retransmitted SYNs reuse the mapping instead of spawning duplicate pending sockets), rewrites inbound dst to (SMOLTCP_IP, mapped port), creates the smoltcp socket listening on the mapped port, and the TX drain identifies the emitting flow **exactly** by its mapped source port and rewrites (src IP, src port) back to the original destination with full checksum recomputation; the ambiguity scans, drop paths, and promotion rejection are deleted. Teardown now frees mappings on every path: closed-connection removal, pending cleanup (Closed state or 120s unestablished cap — previously pending sockets were never reaped), CreateTcpSocket listen failure, and bridge-task death now aborts the smoltcp socket (previously a dead bridge left an Established zombie whose record and port leaked). UDP was audited and is unaffected (full-tuple key + one real socket per flow). Tests: new regression `smoltcp_tcp_bridge_same_client_port_different_destinations` (two flows, same client port, different dst IPs on the same dst port, full handshake + bidirectional data + cross-talk assertions — **fails on the old code at the second flow's SYN-ACK, passes now**), new `rewrite_dst_inbound` unit test, existing packet-by-packet e2e unchanged and green; bonded-server suite 69 passed with only the 2 pre-existing sandbox UDP/ICMP failures and 2 sandbox-hanging wireguard tests (identical on the pristine tree). Bench context: bulk through the tunnel was already healthy for same-destination flows (Pixel 6a: 50 MB at ~41 Mbps; 16×10 MB at ~107 Mbps aggregate), which is why the defect hid from earlier testing. Full investigation: goal checkpoint `fast-reliability-investigation-2026-10-17.md`. Adjacent findings left open: QUIC to the server times out over the home NAT hairpin (sessions silently degrade to WSS-only at home; server logs h3 accept failures for hairpinned connections), the client skips a protocol on remaining paths after it fails on one path in the same establishment round, and the FFI worker drops the in-flight frame on send-failover instead of re-queueing it. **Deployment status (2026-10-18)**: committed as a0d1d3f and deployed to the dell server the same morning, then **rolled back within the hour** after bench verification observed a single wrong-certificate event (a bench Pixel HTTPS connection to speed.cloudflare.com received a \*.googleapis.com certificate through the tunnel on the fixed server). Extensive follow-up the same day could not reproduce any cross-flow mixing: a new churn harness (`smoltcp_forwarder_churn_no_cross_talk` — real smoltcp client stack, interleaved bursts over a small reused client-port pool, aborts, per-flow payload-integrity checks; uncommitted in the working tree) passes 41/41, and a staging deployment of the fixed binary (production ports, filtered copy of production data, tcp bridge dump enabled) passed all bulk matrices with zero TLS errors on the phones. Separately, staging proved a **client session-recovery wedge**: after any server restart, phones auto-recover into a connected-but-blackholed session until a manual VPN toggle — reproduced 6/6 bounces on BOTH the fixed and pre-fix server binaries (goal checkpoint `session-recovery-wedge-2026-10-18.md`), so it is a client (build 137) defect independent of this fix, and it explains the DNS-dead bench runs during the first verification attempt. Production remains on the pre-fix server image (rollback tag `oct2026-prefwd`); redeploying the NAT fix is pending the user's decision.
- **Build 137 \u2014 server-identity verification at session start + dashboard update check (implemented and validated on-device 2026-10-17; rolled out to the Play internal track the same day, v137 completed)**: closes the silent failure behind two phones that connected but could never peer \u2014 their paired records pin a stale server identity (`Pjt73it\u2026`, from an earlier test-server pairing), and nothing in the client checked that pin outside the peer machinery, so the VPN worked while peering failed closed for reasons invisible to the user. The hole was in `resolve_wss_tls_connector`: when the cert-proof bootstrap failed, the WSS path logged a warning and **fell back to system-CA validation**. Changes: (a) cert-proof failures are classified (`cert_proof::is_identity_mismatch_error`) \u2014 a well-formed proof that does not verify against the paired key (bad signature, or signed fingerprint \u2260 presented certificate) is an *identity mismatch*, distinct from reachability failures (unreachable endpoint, malformed response), which say nothing about identity; (b) the WSS connector refuses the CA fallback on identity mismatches (reachability failures keep the fallback, now logged at error level as identity-unverified); (c) `establish_transport_paths` runs an up-front identity gate \u2014 one cert-proof fetch against the bootstrap endpoint under the existing 8s path budget \u2014 whenever a server key is configured, and fails closed on identity evidence from any attempt, including discarding a session a path managed to connect over an unverified transport; the failure is a canonical `Server identity verification failed \u2026 Re-pair this device` error that the FFI surfaces verbatim as the snapshot `lastError` (rendered by the dashboard's persistent error banner) and that the Android service matches to **skip automatic session recovery**, since reconnecting cannot fix a key mismatch. Legitimate ACME certificate rotation is unaffected: the proof re-verifies under the unchanged identity key and the fresh fingerprint is pinned for the session. Tests: bonded-client 42/42 (4 new cert-proof classification unit tests plus `identity_gate_fails_closed_on_wrong_paired_server_key`, an in-process TLS proof-server integration test: wrong pin fails establishment with the canonical error, right pin passes the gate), bonded-core 30/30, bonded-ffi 4/4. On-device validation: `install -r` on both phones, sessions restarted \u2014 the worker logs `server identity verified via cert proof fingerprint=sha256:81a97d05\u2026` at each start, both phones connected with 3 transports and `peerRelayCount=1`, relays bound server-side (`relayed_via_provider=true`). (d) Dashboard UX in the same build, at the user's direction: background status events no longer pop a SnackBar per event (they fired repeatedly after connecting and covered the bottom action button; state remains on the status banner/cards, errors on the persistent banner), and the dashboard checks Play for an update on open (Play Core app-update, IMMEDIATE flow) showing a tappable `Update available (build N)` banner \u2014 silently absent for sideloaded or Play-less installs. Operational note: implicit `am broadcast` VPN_START/STOP does not reach the manifest receiver at targetSdk 36 from a cold process \u2014 use the explicit-component form (recorded in TOOLS.md). Motivating diagnosis: goal checkpoint `stale-identity-pin-2026-10-17.md`.
- **Build 135 — peer rediscovery re-drive + dead-leg detection (implemented and validated on-device 2026-10-17; rolled out to the Play internal track)**: the build-134 soak exposed the next gap in the same flow. Validation: `install -r` on both phones — symmetric relays formed in ~1.5s; at the introductions' expiry the workers retired both relays (21:03:39Z server-side end), the browse tasks re-drove discovery from the cached advertisements, and **new relays were server-bound 5s later on the same sessions (transports 98/99, `relayed_via_provider=true`) with no session restart** — the retire→redial cycle took ~10s end to end. The part (c) stress-test fix was verified in the same session: warm-up negotiated h3 immediately and all 5 rounds passed `protocol=h3` with zero failures (previously rounds 1–2 failed 4 runs out of 4). After a relay path closed and the peer was un-marked ("allowing rediscovery"), no new relay ever formed until a runtime restart: `PeerShareBrowser::recv()` returns only on mdns-sd `ServiceResolved`, which fires **once per service instance**, so the browse task blocked forever waiting for an event that never repeats (this also explains why the overnight outage ended only with an incidental re-resolution). (a) Rediscovery: the browse task now caches the last accepted advertisement per peer and, on a path-close, re-drives discovery from the cache via a bounded `RedialQueue` — a fresh server introduction and re-dial after 10s, at most 3 attempts, so a departed peer cannot cause an introduction-request loop; fresh mDNS resolutions still take the normal path and refresh the cache. Unit coverage: `redial_queue_dedupes_pending_peer`, `redial_queue_exhausts_attempts_then_allows_fresh_enqueue`, `redial_queue_forget_clears_attempt_budget`. (b) Dead-leg detection: the soak also showed a consumer↔provider QUIC leg dying silently (~10:37 PDT) — the provider churned a starving upstream every 30s while the consumer's heartbeat sends kept succeeding into the dead connection until expiry cleanup bounded the loss. The FFI heartbeat tick now also retires non-active peer relays whose QUIC connection has closed (`QuicTransport::connection_closed` → `ClientTransport::peer_relay_connection_closed`), triggering the close-notifier and rediscovery within one tick instead of at introduction expiry. Test status: bonded-core 30/30, bonded-ffi 4/4, bonded-client peer tests + new queue tests pass; the 7 bonded-client UDP/QUIC integration failures are pre-existing sandbox limitations, verified identical on the pristine tree. Full diagnosis: goal checkpoint `rediscovery-gap-2026-10-17.md`. (c) Protocol stress test reliability (same build): the "unreliable" early-round http3 failures were a tester artifact, not corruption — `ProtocolStressTester` built a fresh Cronet engine per run with QUIC enabled but no hint and no persisted state, so the first ~2 rounds necessarily fell back to HTTP/2 while QUIC was discovered via Alt-Svc, and the tester hard-failed any non-h3 completion (reproduced 4/4 runs on-device: 8 of 25 http3 attempts, all in rounds 1–2, identical `protocol='h2'` error; all successful bodies byte-identical — the tunnel and QUIC payloads are exonerated). The tester now seeds the engine with `addQuicHint` for the configured HTTP/3 host and runs one unscored warm-up fetch before the scored rounds, so scored rounds measure steady-state HTTP/3; an h2 completion in a scored round remains a failure, as it then genuinely signals QUIC breakage. Diagnosis: goal checkpoint `http3-stress-investigation-2026-10-17.md`.
- **Build 134 — relay longevity + reconnect fixes (implemented and validated on-device 2026-10-17; shipped as versionCode 134 because the overnight documentation commit consumed the 133 slot in the git-count version formula; also rolled out to the Play internal track)**: addresses the follow-up above from both ends. Validation: `install -r` on both phones, sessions restarted — peer relays formed **symmetrically** within ~1s (each phone's session gained a `relayed_via_provider=true` transport via the other), and the reconnect trap regression test passed: a debug-connect restart issued with the TUN up re-authenticated and re-formed the relay in ~5s with zero 8s deadline failures (on build 132 the same action wedged the VPN until a full service stop/start). (a) Provider: the relay re-registration loop now checks the introduction's `expires_at` before every attempt and abandons the relay once it has expired — dropping the peer connection so the consumer notices promptly — instead of retrying a credential the server will always reject (the 44-attempt storm above). Only the consumer can mint a fresh introduction, so provider-side persistence past expiry is futile by design. (b) Consumer: the FFI worker only ever receives on the active path, so a non-active relay whose provider upstream died was never detected — its QUIC leg stayed healthy, heartbeat sends kept succeeding into the provider's buffers, and the dead relay blocked rediscovery for ~15 minutes (and inflated the reported transport counts). The worker's heartbeat tick now retires any non-active peer-relay transport whose introduction has expired (`ClientTransport::peer_relay_expired`); closing it fires the relay close-notifier, the peer runtime un-marks the peer, and the next advertisement produces a fresh introduction and a new relay. A relay actively carrying traffic is exempt — its failure surfaces through the existing active-path failover. Unit coverage: `introduction_expiry_gate`; the two pre-existing sandbox UDP failures in bonded-client lib tests are unchanged. (c) Reconnect DNS trap (found the same morning on both phones): session restarts that skip `onStartCommand`'s early DNS resolution (session recovery, network rebind, debug connects) handed the native worker the bare server hostname while the TUN was still up; DNS then routed into the dead tunnel, every protocol attempt timed out at 8s, and the VPN could never self-heal (recovery required a full service stop/start). The paired record now persists the last successful resolution (`lastResolvedAddress`, updated only by genuine IP-literal results, never the hostname fallback), and `startNativeSession` prefers fresh early resolution → persisted last-good → raw address. A live build-132 relay also showed the provider upstream does NOT time out while the consumer's 25s heartbeat pings traverse it — the overnight 00:30 drop required a ≥30s relaying stall (device doze/standby on the idle provider is the leading suspect), which is what parts (a)/(b) make recoverable.

- **Build 185 — connect-first app redesign + interception surfacing (2026-10-27)**: at the user's direction the app is reorganized around one-tap connecting. The main screen replaces the server list + per-server dashboard flow: a server **dropdown** (paired servers plus "Add server…"; locked while connected — switching requires disconnecting first, no hot-switch), one large Connect/Disconnect button for the selected server, status + uptime, session totals, per-path rows (network, bind address, protocol, heartbeat RTT, per-path bytes), and rolling graphs (throughput from 1 s counter sampling, active-path RTT). The old dashboard is demoted to a read-only **Session details** page; the stress test / network tests / client logs move behind a **Diagnostics** hub; both are reached from an app-bar menu along with **Settings**, which gains auto-connect toggles (on app open, on boot via `BootReceiver`). A **Quick Settings tile** and a **home-screen widget** toggle the selected server without opening the app (opened via `AppSettingsStore`, a SharedPreferences store shared by Dart, tile, widget, and boot receiver; without VPN consent both open the app instead). Native plumbing: the FFI session snapshot gains per-path stats (`path_stats`: path index, kind, active flag, per-path byte counters incremented at the existing send/recv sites, per-path last RTT — all paths are pinged on the same heartbeat tick, so a pong's arrival transport owns the RTT) and the Kotlin snapshot/JSON forwards them; `peerShareError` is also forwarded now (the old dashboard read it from a map that never contained it). **Interception surfacing (same build, user request after the 2026-10-27 Comcast wrong-certificate sighting)**: the pinned-TLS verifier's rejection did not carry the `server-identity-mismatch` marker, so a wrong certificate on a path (the fingerprint flavor of verification) was treated as an ordinary network failure instead of identity evidence — the connection was still refused by the verifier, but the session did not fail closed *as an identity failure* and nothing surfaced it. The verifier now emits the marker (legitimate rotations are unaffected: the in-connect rotation refresh re-proves the presented cert against the paired key first). `establish_single_transport_path` additionally emits a machine-readable observer marker naming the path; the FFI worker records it in the snapshot as an `interception` record (path index, detail, timestamp), the path supervisor's `SessionDead` outcome carries the dialed path index for the same attribution on re-dial failures, and the app shows a dedicated "Possible traffic interception" warning naming the connection (Wi-Fi/Cellular + bind address), dismissible per occurrence, with full detail on the Details page. New regression test: `pinned_verifier_rejection_is_an_identity_mismatch`. Baselines held: bonded-client targeted identity/establish tests pass (full suite still has only the sandbox UDP/QUIC set), bonded-ffi 11 passed / 1 known CryptoProvider failure, `flutter analyze` clean.
- **Build 186 — per-path public (egress) IP + update-button removal (2026-10-27)**: completes the build-185 design's one dropped element — egress IP per path, server-reported as designed. The server now stamps every heartbeat/announcement PONG payload with the source address it observes for that transport connection (`bonded_core::session::encode_observed_address` / `decode_observed_address`); all six session-loop pong sites are stamped (bootstrap WSS loop, main generic loop, QUIC loop — initial + heartbeat each). The encoding is backward compatible both ways: PINGs are empty-payload by definition and older servers echo an empty payload (client shows nothing), while older clients ignore PONG payloads entirely. The FFI session loop decodes the stamp on each pong and records it per path in the snapshot (`serverSeenAddress`, preserved across stats republishes like the byte counters); the WireGuard session loop is the one unstamped exception — its peer struct does not expose the boringtun endpoint — so WG paths simply show no public IP until that is plumbed. The app shows "Public IP x.x.x.x" (port stripped) under each path row on the main screen and the full "IP:port — server sees" row on Session details. Same build removes the manual **Check for Updates** button at the user's direction: the automatic Play check (app open + foreground return, 30 min throttle) and its "update available" banner remain. New test: `observed_address_roundtrip_and_legacy_payloads` (core). Baselines held: core session tests pass, server `transport_session_delivers_*` pong-path tests pass, bonded-ffi 11/1 known baseline, `flutter analyze` clean. The app half works against the old server (field absent); the public IP appears once the production server runs the stamped build.

## Deferred to Later Phases

These are in v1 scope but depend on the above being stable first:

- **ShadowSocks transport** — deferred until WSS and QUIC are stable and validated in production; CR-8 lists it as "v1 if feasible" but the effort is not justified before the primary transports are proven
- **Bootstrap API rate limiting** — per-IP and per-token rate limits on `/v1/bootstrap/*` endpoints; deferred until bootstrap API is implemented and attack surface is better understood; mitigated in the interim by invite token single-use semantics and device keypair binding
- **Peer-initiated bootstrap (future)** — a client with no working direct Internet path establishes its session entirely through a LAN peer: relayed server-signed introduction and cert-proof over a pre-introduction bootstrap stream, plus relay-first session creation on the server. Design note: `docs/design/peer-bootstrap-relay.md`
- **Battery optimization (AND-6)**
- **Quick settings tile (AND-7)**

---

## Key Implementation Decisions

Decisions made during implementation that aren't in the requirements docs.

| Decision | Date | Notes |
|----------|------|-------|
| Reverse-dial signaling: server-mediated push on the provider's live session, no queueing (4.56) | 2026-11-07 | Alternatives were (a) provider polling — a new loop + latency for an event the server already observes; (b) speculative dial-back on discovering a consumer advertisement — dials peers that may not want a leg; (c) piggyback on heartbeat PONGs — true old-client invisibility but 3 PONG sites + a pending store + up-to-25 s idle latency. Chosen: direct push via the existing session router (`requeue`), dropped when the provider has no live session; the consumer's next introduction request re-pushes. |
| Reversed-introduction direction is part of the server-signed claims (4.56) | 2026-11-07 | `direction: forward|reverse` on the claims; both forward verifiers reject reversed introductions and both reversed verifiers reject forward ones, so neither direction replays as the other. The server registration check accepts both directions — direction governs the peer leg, not the server binding, so `relayed_via_provider` accounting is identical. |
| Dial-back fields excluded from the introduction request's signed payload (4.56) | 2026-11-07 | The signed payload covers only the original six fields so a new client still verifies against an old server (which verifies with its own six-field payload). The dial-back fields ride inside the TLS bootstrap request and the server acts on them only after the core signature verifies. |
| Old-client push invisibility: PONG-bit cloak rejected (4.56) | 2026-11-07 | Verified by reading both inbound loops that old clients do NOT ignore unknown frame types (Android writes the payload to TUN, Linux buffers it in the reorder gap). A PONG-flagged notification would be skipped by both loops but would also write a bogus `last_rtt_ms` into path stats and corrupt transport grading, so the push uses a plain `FLAG_PEER_DIAL_BACK` frame with the session's connection id and sequence u64::MAX. |
| Reverse-leg dedup: leg-tracker dedup by (peer, path) across directions, not a tie-break (4.57) | 2026-11-07 | Chosen (b) over (a) deterministic tie-break (only the lexicographically-lower device key initiates): in the hotspot case the forward direction is known-dead, so a tie-break would strand the consumer if the designated initiator's direction is the broken one, and would need timeout/fallback complexity to recover. Dedup is two layers: provider side, `RelayPathClaims` shared by the forward accept loop and the dial-back handler, with `plan_dial_back` claiming BEFORE dialing — at most one leg per (peer, provider path) in either direction; consumer side, `ConnectedPeers` + `note_reversed_leg` — the accept loop refuses a reversed leg for an already-connected peer with `no_path` before sending `ok`, and the browse loop drops any that arrives anyway. The consumer dedups by peer only (it cannot know which provider path a reversed leg is pinned to); the path-level guarantee lives on the provider. Tradeoff: a reversed leg for a peer with an existing forward leg is dropped even when it would have added a path — forward dial-until-`no_path` already claims all paths when the provider is dialable. |
| Consumer accept: reversed introduction verifies against the consumer's own listener identity; expected provider comes from the server-signed claims (4.57) | 2026-11-07 | On a reversed leg the listener IS the consumer, so `verify_reversed_introduction_for_listener` checks consumer key == local device key and fingerprint == own listener cert. The expected provider is the one the server signed into the claims rather than a locally-pinned value: the server signature is the trust root for which provider the consumer asked to be dialed back by, and the server only mints a reversed introduction for a provider this consumer requested dial-back for. The forward path differs because there the listener is the provider, so its own key is the check. |
| Dial-back frame intercept: both session workers, binding + steady-state; supervisor bind left as retryable (4.57) | 2026-11-07 | Shared `peer_runtime::forward_dial_back_frame` is called on every inbound session frame BEFORE the PONG check and the data path, in the Linux packet loop and the Android FFI worker, at both the steady-state receive arm and the session-binding PONG wait (a notification arriving mid-binding is forwarded and the binding ping re-sent instead of failing session start — the push can arrive any time after auth). The path-supervisor re-dial bind is deliberately NOT wired: a dial-back frame there is a retryable bind failure (supervisor re-dials; the consumer's next introduction cycle re-pushes). |
| Battery profiler: charge counter is whole-device drain — attribution is differential and state-segmented, never an absolute "Bonded used X mAh" (4.46) | 2026-11-05 | Rates show window length + sample count; degrade to level-% deltas on devices without a usable counter and say so; Doze sparsity is expected, not a bug. |
| Battery profiler: sample on the existing session-monitor tick only — no new timers, alarms, or WorkManager work (4.46) | 2026-11-05 | A sample is BatteryManager/PowerManager reads + one /proc scan + arithmetic. Raw samples in-memory only; hourly aggregates persist (7 days, capped file). |
| Battery profiler: hand-rolled fixed-schema JSONL codec for hourly aggregates (4.46) | 2026-11-05 | `org.json` android.jar stubs throw in local unit tests and a new dependency for one small file is not worth it; decoder drops malformed lines. |
| Battery profiler: plumb new counters end-to-end (Rust → Kotlin → Dart) with a test at each layer (4.46) | 2026-11-05 | Lesson from the discovered 4.41 gap: Kotlin never forwards `wireTxBytes`/`wireRxBytes`, so the overhead card's wire rows can only ever show "not measured" on a real device. `quicPingFrames` was plumbed all the way through to avoid repeating that miss. |
| Session grade: battery excluded from the score (4.47) | 2026-11-06 | User-directed: no mAh/h in the grade, no battery data wired into the grader. The 4.46 profiler + Battery card are untouched — battery data exists, it just isn't scored. Weights went 30/30/20/20 → 35/35/30, then bandwidth took the freed 20. |
| Session grade: bandwidth = median combined throughput over active windows only (4.47) | 2026-11-06 | User-directed: bandwidth is a factor. A window counts only when bytes moved in it (the per-window form of the 60 s idle definition), so idle stretches never drag the score down. Bands: 100 at ≥ 10 Mbps, 0 at ≤ 0.1 Mbps, linear — a coarse network-independent scale for explainability, not a calibrated baseline. |
| Session grade: REMOVED 2026-11-17 (was: accumulate in Dart from the snapshot stream; medians from histogram bands, 4.47) | 2026-11-06 | Tracker fed on the home screen's existing poll tick — no new timers, wakeups, or snapshot fields. Bounded histograms (one counter per bucket); the card shows the band, never a fake-precise median. Intervals > 35 s skipped as unknown time; flaps between samples are missed at the 30 s background cadence. No session-history store in this slice: live grade + retained last-session grade only. | REMOVED: the session-grade card is gone (Tom's decision); the histogram/median machinery now lives in `grade_scoring.dart` for the per-transport grader only.
| QUIC loss: loss rate = lost_packets / sent_packets from quinn PathStats; Option<u64> in WireStats so non-QUIC reports null, not 0 (4.49) | 2026-11-06 | A lost QUIC packet is retransmitted by quinn, so `lost_packets` is effectively the retransmit count. `Option<u64>` (not 0) for the loss fields: 0 would be a fabricated "no loss" claim for transports that cannot observe it (TCP, WireGuard); the UI shows "—" for those. Null also while sent is zero. |
| Snapshot parse/forward extracted to testable top-level functions; org.json as a test-only dependency (4.48) | 2026-11-06 | `parseSessionPaths` + the per-path map builder were private members of `BondedVpnService`, which cannot be instantiated in a local unit test; extracting them to top-level `internal` functions in the same file makes the parse + forward unit-testable without changing behavior. The parse test needs the real org.json — android.jar stubs throw in local unit tests — so `testImplementation("org.json:json:20240303")`; test scope only, never ships. (Differs from the 4.46 aggregates-codec decision, which avoided a new dep for production code; this dep is test-only.) |
| Transport grade: reuse 4.47 scoring primitives; bandwidth = 100 unless saturation observed (4.50) | 2026-11-06 | User-directed: grades belong on the transports, not the session. The 4.47 session-grade card was REMOVED 2026-11-17 (Tom's decision). Weights 30/30/20/20 (latency/stability/loss/bandwidth) — loss takes availability's 20, since per-path availability is meaningless (a path may legitimately sit idle while another carries the session). Latency/stability functions, letters, rebalancing total, and the RTT histogram buckets are reused from `grade_scoring.dart` (moved there when the session grade was removed) (histogram helpers promoted private → public); only loss + bandwidth scoring are new. Loss: 100 at ≤ 0.1%, 0 at ≥ 10%, linear, gated on ≥ 100 packets sent; non-QUIC never scores loss. Bandwidth does NOT reuse the 4.47 absolute-throughput bands — those penalize light use. Score = 100 × (1 − saturated/active windows), gated on ≥ 10 active windows; saturated = RTT inflation vs the path's own low-water mark (≥ 1.5× and ≥ +100 ms) or an interval loss spike (≥ 5% over ≥ 20 packets). Plateau-while-demand-exists is NOT implemented: no demand signal exists in the snapshot (no per-path queue depth), so a plateau is indistinguishable from light use. |
| Transport grade display: chip on the path row, tap for detail (4.50) | 2026-11-06 | Letter + score chip on each direct-path row and each peer-relay leg, next to the Active chip; tap opens a bottom-sheet detail with the four sub-scores + measurements (mirrors the session-grade card rows). Long-press keeps the debug menu (pause/reset) — tap and long-press are separate gestures. No chip and no sheet while a path has no grade yet, never a fabricated number. |
| Traffic modes: rules live in Rust, Dart owns persistence + UI + resolution (4.51) | 2026-11-06 | Alternatives were (a) keep rules in Rust with CRUD over FFI, (b) keep rules in Dart and push them per packet. Chosen: the rule table lives in Rust (`TrafficModeEngine`, global `RwLock` in bonded-ffi) because matching runs on the packet path and must never cross the JNI boundary per packet; Dart owns persistence (SharedPreferences via the existing AppSettings channel), the UI, and DNS pre-resolution, and hands Rust the whole table as one JSON blob at session start plus live updates via `nativeUpdateTrafficRules`. Kotlin is a string passthrough (never parses the JSON). Malformed JSON falls back to the default engine rather than failing the session. |
| Domain rules pre-resolve on-device; resolver-dependence (4.51) | 2026-11-06 | Proven the same evening: `listen.mixlr.com` resolves to 15.235.46.15 on the user's network but 198.18.232.237 from this VM's resolver (DNS load balancing / geo-DNS). Consequences, all locked in: the rule MUST be resolved on-device with the device's own resolver (never a build-time or sandbox-time resolution); it must match ALL IPs the resolver returns, not just the first; it must re-resolve on VPN start; a failed re-resolution keeps the stored IPs; a destination outside the resolved set falls back to the default mode (never captured by a stale rule). Neither observed IP is hardcoded anywhere in app code; tests use the user's observed IP only as literal input data to the matcher. A rule whose domain never resolved matches nothing and is flagged in the UI. |
| Mode fallback + ordering ownership (4.51) | 2026-11-06 | `mode_for` returns the SELECTED mode (raw) and `order_candidates` applies the fallback to default ordering — keeping selection and fallback separate is what makes the specificity rule testable before 4.52–4.54 implement the modes. The UI states the fallback next to every mode picker (with the implementing slice number). Ordering ownership split: the core engine owns the try-list order, the FFI worker only demotes paused paths (`demote_paused_paths` replaces `order_candidates_for_send`, whose hash rotation moved into the engine) — so 4.52–4.54 are pure additions of ordering arms, not refactors. |
| Best-transport ranking rule (4.52) | 2026-11-06 | Grade first (4.50 total, higher wins), then RTT (lower wins), then loss rate (lower wins); a missing signal at any step sorts after any measured one — unknown is never good and never a fabricated 0 — and the transport index breaks remaining ties so the order is deterministic for a given signal set. That determinism is the pinning: a flow stays on its first candidate while the transport set and the signals are stable, with no per-packet re-pinning and no per-flow state in the engine. Degrades gracefully as signals arrive: no grades yet → RTT decides; no RTT yet either → loss decides; nothing measured → index order (every flow starts on transport 0 until the first heartbeat pong or grade push). Lowest latency is a SEPARATE arm, not a re-skin: RTT alone, grade and loss never enter the comparison, so a high-grade slow path loses to a low-grade fast one. |
| Return flows follow the last-used transport (4.52) | 2026-11-06 | The server router records flow → transport on every uplink data frame (`note_frame` now takes the frame, not just flags) and `deliver` sends the reply back on that transport when it is still live, falling back to the existing hash pin when the flow has no uplink data yet. Matching directions needs a direction-independent key: `flow_hash` is per-direction (src/dst in order), so a new `bonded-core` `flow_key` hashes the two endpoint (address, port) pairs in canonical order — uplink and reply share a key, different flows do not. The map is bounded at 4096 entries (arbitrary single-entry eviction when full; the evicted flow re-learns on its next uplink packet) and pruned on `remove_transport` / `adopt_epoch` / `retire_by_peer`. PING/PONG frames never claim a flow (guarded in `note_frame`); unparseable packets have no key and fall back to hash pinning. The non-multipath `deliver` branch (last-active first) is unchanged. |
| Grades cross the JNI boundary on the existing poll tick (4.52) | 2026-11-06 | The 4.50 grades live in Dart, but best transport ranks in Rust — so Dart pushes the grade totals to Rust on the home screen's existing 1 s poll tick via a new `PathGradesService` (`updatePathGrades`, bare JSON array in snapshot paths-list order, one total per transport vec index, null when ungraded) through MainActivity → `BondedVpnService.updatePathGrades` → `nativeUpdatePathGrades` JNI; Kotlin is a string passthrough like the rule table and never parses the JSON. Rust keeps them in a global `RwLock<Vec<Option<u8>>>`; the worker builds `PathSignal`s per packet from the snapshot (heartbeat RTT, QUIC loss rate) plus the grades by vec index. Session start clears the grades so a new session never ranks on the previous session's paths before the first push; malformed JSON clears rather than keeping stale grades. The Linux client has no grades (grade `None`) and no rule input, so best transport there ranks on RTT/loss only — CLI rule input is still open from 4.51. |
| Bandwidth-boost weighting rule (4.53) | 2026-11-06 | `weight = (grade / 100) * (1 - loss_rate)`, grade the 4.50 total, loss the 4.49 QUIC rate clamped to [0, 1]; RTT is deliberately excluded (latency belongs to `lowest_latency`). The flow hash picks a transport with probability proportional to its weight (`flow_hash % 1_000_000` slot into [0, total weight)), so flows stay pinned to one transport — flow-level spreading, NOT per-packet striping — and the pick is deterministic in (flow_hash, weights) with no per-flow engine state. Unknown signals are neutral, never perfect and never zero: unknown grade → 50, unknown loss → 5% (non-finite counts as unknown), so an unmeasured path weighs 0.475; with nothing measured the spread is uniform, i.e. boost with no data is the default spreading. A fully-lossy (loss >= 1) or zero-grade transport weighs 0 and never leads a new flow but stays last in the try-list as failover; all-zero weights fall back to the exact default hash spread. The return half needs no server change: the 4.52 router records flow → transport on every uplink data frame regardless of mode, so a boost flow's return traffic follows its weighted-picked transport automatically. |
| Redundant mode: flag signaling + fan-out both directions (4.54) | 2026-11-06 | SIGNALLING: the server has no rule table, so the client signals redundancy with one bit — FLAG_REDUNDANT (0x0040) on its outbound data frames, a bit on frames already sent, no new control message and no rule sync. Old servers ignore unknown flag bits (new client degrades to single-transport return against them). The router records redundancy per flow in the SAME bounded 4096-entry map as the last-used transport (`FlowReturn { transport, redundant }` — one bound, one eviction, one prune) and refreshes it on every uplink data frame, so a live rule change takes effect on the flow's next uplink packet; PING/PONG never mark a flow. CLIENT FAN-OUT: a redundant flow is not pinned — duplication replaces pinning. The engine's redundant arm returns every transport in index order; the pin points fan out to the healthy prefix of the demoted list, so paused paths (debug pause and battery-saver auto-pause alike) never receive a duplicate, all-paused falls back to the ordinary try-list (packet goes out once, never dropped), and one transport sends exactly one copy. A failed target is removed with the usual bookkeeping and the fan-out continues; the Linux client loop runs the same branch (inert under its default engine until CLI rule input lands) so the two pin points cannot drift. RECEIVING SIDE / DEDUP: no new dedup layer anywhere, stated honestly — the Android worker writes every received frame payload straight to TUN (no session reorder/dedup, by design), so duplicate downlink packets reach the kernel twice: TCP dedups in-kernel by sequence, UDP duplicates reach the app. Server-side the same holds through smoltcp (TCP dedups, UDP delivers twice). The Linux client is the exception: its single shared SessionState drops the second copy while buffered or as stale after delivery — and stale is now a benign drop, not a session-ending error, because a fanned-out downlink's second copy routinely arrives after the first was consumed. COST: N× bandwidth on every duplicated flow, both directions; session counters count the user packet once, per-path counters show the N× cost. GATING: server fan-out requires multipath_capable (an old client only reads its active transport); the frame fanned out is the same frame on every transport (same sequence, clone per target except the last takes the original). |
| Widget-test Clipboard mock must use JSONMethodCodec on `flutter/platform` (4.46) | 2026-11-05 | `Clipboard.setData` never completes unmocked (no engine handler); `setMockMethodCallHandler` on `flutter/platform` breaks the framework's own calls; decode with JSONMethodCodec, not StandardMethodCodec (the platform channel uses it). Mock answers only `Clipboard.setData`, returns null for everything else. |
| Relayed-byte counter naming and direction (4.45) | 2026-11-05 | `relayed_up_bytes` / `relayedUpBytes` = toward the server (consumer -> server through the provider); `relayed_down_bytes` / `relayedDownBytes` = toward the peer/consumer (server -> consumer). Same ↓/↑ orientation as the Totals row. Counts are session-frame payload bytes on successful forward — the unit the pump throttles on — not wire bytes, so they exclude QUIC/TLS framing overhead. One aggregate per runtime (all legs share it); no per-peer accounting in this slice. Counters are per-session (fresh runtime + fresh snapshot per session start). The snapshot holds the shared `Arc` and reads it live at JSON time rather than copying on a tick, so displayed totals can never go stale between polls. |
| Tunnel DNS uses a single required `dns_server`, blind-forwarded verbatim | 2026-11-04 | The proxy is a dumb forwarder to one operator-chosen upstream; no /etc/resolv.conf fallback, no gateway guessing, no public default, no resolver list. Redundancy/caching/LAN zones belong in the operator's own resolver. This closes the systemd-stub single-label trap (127.0.0.53 will not forward single-label names to unicast DNS). |

| UDP forwarding now uses per-client-session long-lived flow sockets with 4-minute idle expiry | 2026-04-06 | Each UDP 4-tuple creates/reuses a connected ephemeral socket; server pushes all remote datagrams back to client asynchronously until no client packet is seen for 4 minutes |

Session 30 cleanup: `frame_forwarder.rs` deleted; `main.rs` concurrency_tests rewritten without legacy TcpFlowTable; `UdpSessionManager` now holds `Arc<TokioMutex<HashMap<UdpFlowKey, UdpFlowHandle>>>` so each UDP 4-tuple reuses one OS socket across packets; UDP response frames carry client `connection_id` from request; `bonded-core` `Default` impls converted to `#[derive(Default)]`; clippy clean (`-D warnings`) on bonded-server; all 32 integration/unit tests pass. Known limitation: ICMP still uses one DGRAM socket per probe — per-session shared socket deferred because DGRAM ICMP sockets receive kernel-assigned echo identifiers that differ from client IDs, making demultiplexing ambiguous without a separate mapping layer. |

| smoltcp bridge remote→client path now preserves partial write tails across polls | 2026-04-30 | `send_slice` may accept fewer bytes than requested when socket send buffer has limited room; forwarder now stores unsent remainder per TCP connection and retries later so server-originated payload bytes are not silently dropped between bridge logs and tunnel output |
